Today, we are in a break-up between a demanding operation in Afghanistan – and an increasingly demanding phase of it – and more focused on domestic defence tasks. Nevertheless, we should always be prepared to participate in foreign operations. Fresh experience shows that the mandate and request for this can be speeded up. I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation on a major and important matter. The proposition addresses a wide range of central themes, searing the main weight both there and in the setting naturally on the Air Force. Swivel if the long-term planning period is four years, the decisions taken tonight will carry for many decades to come. Mainly in this context, the Committee has agreed to the largest acquisition of defence in modern times and the largest investment in mainland Norway and has agreed to the acquisition of up to 52 F-35 fighter aircraft. An overall committee is also at the forefront of security and defence policy, which states that Norway is anchored in NATO and that transatlantic relations are essential to our security. There is a broad consensus in the Committee on Ørland as the main base for fighter aircraft, and that it should be a permanent, forward-looking operating base in North Norway. A Joint Committee takes important steps to reform the Army and facilitate a modern, robust and forward-looking cyber defence. Furthermore, a Joint Committee expresses clear expectations for a completely required reform of personnel and competences in the defence sector, which will be a fundamental prerequisite for future defence and for soldiers, officers and civil servants. So we're going to be together a lot. We have to share that with each other. At the same time, what is real, material and important disagreements must not be hidden. While we largely agree on what defence we want, at the same time we disagree on what defence we really have today. We also disagree on whether the government's proposals bring us closer to the defence we should have. The Government claims that there is a balance between tasks, structure and resources, and that this balance is continued in their proposal for a long-term plan. No matter how much it had pleased me that this actually voted, right, along with the rest of the opposition, that the government's image does not match reality. This is a fundamental difference, in principle, in how to deal with the further activities of the defence. This is what is at the heart of the political disagreement about defence, not about localisation. This is where the debate should be conducted. From the Right we want a debate on just these things, but it has proved to be difficult. There are several reasons for this. The government, in turn, has provided a weak and partly inadequate basis for decision-making to the Storting in an important defence case. The proposal contains proposals for decisions that will have a significant impact on defence, on employees and on more communities in Norway, both in the short and long term. Neither premise, discretion nor consequences are made visible and adequately justified. In this way, as far as this proposition is concerned, it is a practice of the ministry. It is not acceptable to have such a poor decision-making basis for Stortinget, as the opposition has mentioned in detail in the setting. Such a decisive premise for the long term as the economic planning assumptions are described above two sides of the proposition. When the government cannot or will not provide answers to entirely fundamental economic and operational questions in its proposals, they make it difficult for the sea to gather the necessary support. There are a wide range of issues that should be debated. How do we ensure a solid and forward-looking reform of personnel and competences that a total increase is behind? How do we ensure even better follow-up of our veterans? This evening, the biggest acquisition ever in the Army, the new armored vehicle, will be worth 10 billion. kr. There's a bunch of pussies in the back. We should discuss the realism of operating the new maritime defence ship structure with new and important capacities within a flat operating budget. How could we get far more military capability out of Homeland than we do today? We should also discuss the concerns related to the operating costs of the elements proposed by the Defence Manager, and the government proposed to maintain. These costs run from day one in the planning period, while the money spent on the defence budget to contribute to this coverage will only be fully effective in the last year of the planning period. An even more important issue is the long-term sustainability of the structure, the factors referred to by the head of defence in his principal opinion as ‘demanding’, or a defence structure described by the FFI as fragile and marginal — not least in view of the share of the costs of the acquisition of fighter aircraft that must now be taken within the defence budget. That's going to increase by between 9.5 billion. kr and 15,5 brd. kr. State councillor Espen Barth Eide accuses today's VG opposition of running tactical games. In addition, the State Secretary of the Council of State claims that the opposition is lying and leads the voters behind the light – because we insist on being factually in disagreement with the government! These are characteristics I think government officials should stay too good at. I still choose to focus on the positive. I would like to commend the government for the work it has started on thinking about a comprehensive personnel and competence reform in the defence sector. In the light of changing societal and demographic trends, there is no doubt that defence needs to be adapted to a new challenge and time in this area as well. It must lead to an overall review of the order system and the current regime for branch boards. From the Right, we want a quick clarification of the question of a newly-designed command system according to NATO standards, which opens up to re-grown specialists. We support such a realignment. There must also be a much greater diversity in the recruitment of defence, and better solutions must be created. The interaction with civil society must be strengthened and deepened. Normalisation must be a crossword, while the professional-military profession must be pure-bred where necessary. In the long term, this reform is probably even more important than the military aircraft acquisition. The soldiers – the humans – are our most important resource. The Committee has wished to recognise this in the form that the Committee may, by means of comments and through the design of the nomination, place the Head of Staff and Competences first among the sections relating to organisation and operational capability – even before the defence sectors. It's a symbolic but equally very important action. The veterans of Defense also constitute a resource – they are the same people, the same soldiers after the end of service. There has been a considerable effort to strengthen the follow-up and recognition of the veterans of Defence in recent years, and I also want to commend the government for that. Important work is done, and important work remains. 27 of the 126 action points of the action plan have been implemented. At the same time, it is important to further improve the work of veterans. The majority of the Committee, i.e. The Progress Party, the Right Party and the Christian People's Party, will do this by looking at the possibility of a new, simplified and improved service-based arrangement for economic compensation, rather than plunging further into today's unbridled amount of substitution-based schemes. We also ask that we look at how civil governance can be organised according to the "one-door" principle. The core of the defence's mission is to provide operational capability. In the long-term plan process, the right has been accused of prioritising asphalt to operational capability. That's not right. Overall, our proposals will improve the operational capacity of defence to a greater extent than the government's proposals. Our proposal to equip the Heimeverten will mean a large increase in rural military capability – the government will cut in the top end of the Home Guard, the forces of effort, and continue an excessive structure with too little training. The maintenance of CRC Mågerø ensures the necessary toughness and redundancy for a very important function in a vulnerable system. These are clear technical arguments for two CRCs. Right wants in the long run three mechanized manoeuvring battalions in the Army. Making the right changes in order and pace, and following solid and verifiable analyses, creates anchoring and predictability in the organisation. It can reduce loss of competence and also reduce costs. In fact, at a time when defence is already under financial pressure, saving hundreds of millions in removal and investment costs over the next few years — in addition to avoiding loss of skills and increasing commuting costs — is a point. All these proposals will increase the operational capability of Defence. Since 2008, the right has proposed a total increase of 2.65 billion in defence budgets. kr, so that more can be flown, sailed more and practiced more. We have shown – and we show in this setting – both the ability to convert and the willingness to pay. The acquisition of combat lye is the overwhelming single factor in the future functioning of the defence – both operationally and economically. Sickness if the acquisition is still associated with a degree of uncertainty both economically and in time, this matter is so important that the Right will contribute to the creation of consensus and predictability for the acquisition and for the sustainability of the defence in the light of it. The Chief of Defence points out in clear order the need to maintain the ambition of 52 aeroplanes in order to ensure the defence's ability to perform all assigned tasks. It is therefore with some wonder that we have noticed the government's proposal that over time can contribute to unnecessary uncertainty about the acquisition and level of ambition. Decisions in the Storting in front of each acquisition are a breach of normal acquisition practices, and an invitation to periodic rematch and rebalancing through cuts in numbers. The reasons given by the government, namely that this means greater political control and the ability to deal with changing assumptions in the project, do not change this picture. The size shall also, as a matter of fact, be continuously and thoroughly informed of any changes to the project by means of a normal procurement procedure. Despite the importance of the fighter planes, the chief of defense recommended an additional 37.5 billion in his professional military council. kr for the acquisition. Instead, the government is adding an extra allocation of 22 billion. kr – 28 br. kr. The increase in the share of the additional costs included in the defence framework will have a major impact on the structure and ability of the defence in other areas. The Minister of Defense says that the planes have to stand somewhere, and he has said it quite correctly. Right-wing support, like a broad majority in the Committee, Ørland as the main base for fighter aircraft. From an overall assessment, Ørland is considered best suited for bringing together the main part of the Air Force Production. In addition, the right will have a permanent, forward base further north, but unlike the government we want a solution where it – given important assumptions – is sought established in Bodø. The government has in its calculations provided that the entire bill of approx. Three billion. kr to move the runway in Bodø is added to the Defence. Based on an overall assessment, the Right believes that there should be a solution based on contributions from all relevant actors, including the Defence, who, in our view, cannot expect to leave Bodø free of charge or with financial gain, as the government's alternative suggests. These factors will determine whether the Bodø option is feasible. It's a clear assumption for the Right that the defense won't be stuck with the bill alone. Together with the Christian People's Party, we have therefore promoted the proposal: ‘The government asks the Government to find a solution where an advanced operating base for fighter aircraft in North Norway is established in Bodø.’ The sum of the proposals for the Rygge Military Air Station is poorly justified and supported. As long as it is chosen to maintain HV-01 and helicopter operations on standby, it will result in a significant need for support functions with associated infrastructure and area. South Norway’s largest ammunition warehouse, the only landing and distribution point for military fuel, and extensive communications and ICT infrastructure are located on Rygge. These are functions that need to be continued, but the cost and arrangements for this are not visible. Therefore, the right will have a much more detailed analysis of the feasibility, impact and cost analysis and the whole of the proposals, including the possible input of other activities. Right is and has always been willing to make difficult decisions to make changes in the Defence. We have shown this for a long time – not least when we were in government from 2001 to 2005. But then there has to be talk about the right transitions, which give real and measurable operational gains or economic savings. In addition, they must be based on sound analyses and verifiable economic and professional premises. In our opinion, the government's proposals do not meet these conditions. I would like to take up with that the proposal on which the Right is the co-rapporteur.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e310	In a lecture in Oslo Military Society in 1995, the then Chief of Defense Arne Solli presented a new defence system with major changes in the defence structure. Among other things, he said:\t\t You ‘It is not just an adaptation of the structure of war and peace to the changing security policy assumptions and economic framework, but a complete reorganization of our way of operating the defence’s overall operations.’ The recognition that the defense we had during the Cold War had to be reorganized was being seriously reorganized, and we had had a defense study and a defence commission. According to Defense Chief Solli, there was a need for a 'complete reorganization'. That was a clear speech! In St. prp. No 45 for 2000-2001 was originally called: ‘The defence is in a deep and sustained structural crisis. Two fundamental imbalances characterise the situation: the size of the defence is out of proportion to the resources it is allocated and the organisation of the defence is not suitable for the task of the future.» It can't be said more clearly. The problems of Norwegian defence in putting relevant units on short notice to NATO's Kosovo actions became a wake-up call. We just didn't have a defense that was adapted to the time. Something had to be done, and the biggest reorganization in the public sector over time was in progress. We got St.prp. No 45 in 2001, St.prp. No 42 in 2004, St.prp. No 48 in 2008 – and now Prop. 73 S in 2012, the fourth long-term plan we are modernizing the Defense. Now we're approaching the end of the basic transition that started 10 to 15 years ago. The defense has changed from a ‘cold war-invading defense’ to a modern defense of intervention. The public sector must constantly change — so will the defence, so there will certainly be changes in the future, but not as far-reaching changes as the defence has been over the last few years. There is reason to commend those who have been involved in the major changes: the management of the defence and all the employees of the defence, both military, civil and military, and first-time soldiers. But I will also praise changing governments and changing numbers of bigots for the job that is done, and as it says: Creeping should be a good listener, for it comes from the heart! In many areas, the main device of the Defence is placed. In 2008, Stortinget decided that the operating headquarters should be added to Retan. The army is solidly established with two heavy footprints, one in the south and one in the north. The maritime defence has renewed the ship structure and has been brought together in the West. The Coast Guard is established in Blackland and is world leader in its class. Now required changes in Air Force are underway, especially the location of our future fighter aircraft. Air defence has not undergone the same structural changes as the Army and Naval Defense have done. It was therefore gratifying that, when examining the previous long-term plan in 2008, the Defence Committee unanimously stated that it was necessary to make changes to the air defence structure with fewer bases. This I will come back to. There has been a broad historical consensus on the main lines of foreign, defence and security policy in Norway. There is agreement on most of it also in this long-term plan, but the policy is now such that it is the areas where there is disagreement, there is attention, and not the areas where there is broad consensus. So also in this plan. Norway must always have the means to combat pressure, aggression or estimation. With modern capabilities and high response capabilities, the Defence shall constitute a war-preventing threshold, entailing high risk and cost to the challenger of Norwegian security and seaworthiness. That's why we have the Defense. When dealing with defence issues, it may seem that we have the Defense because it is important for the community where the Defense is located. Of course, the defence is important to many communities, but the defence must never become an advanced form of district development funds. We don't have the defense bases and garrisons where it's best for the communities – it's Sørreisa, Mågerø, Rygge, Bodø, Ørland, Bardu or Rena. We're going to have them where it's best for the defense. I understand that this can be hard to deal with for some, but that must be the way it is. In the examination of the long-term plan in 2001, there was a discussion on Haslemoen, Hvalsmoen, Eggemoen, Helgelandsmoen, Evjemoen, Fredrikstad, Lahaugmoen, Stavern, Karljohansvern, Torpo, Gråkallen and Kongsvinger. In 2008, there was a discussion about Jåttå, Sola and Bømoen. What do those who wanted to maintain all these camps say today? I haven't met anyone who would suggest that we reestablish the Haslemo, the Helgelandsmoen or the Lahaugmoen – even then discuss it was to lay down these camps a few years back. In my childhood, we had NIKE battery in Nes. It was right to establish these batteries during the cold war at the end of the 1950s, but it was also right to put them down 20 years ago. There are many examples. What kind of defense would we have had if we hadn't made such structural changes? The answer is seaworthy. The interesting thing about structural changes is that the opponents agree with them just a little bit of time has passed. The changes in 2001 were, for example, Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to begin by thanking the President-in-Office of the Council for his excellent report. Similarly, there were protests against the changes made in 2004. The Progress Party then proposed that you keep the structure from 2001 – which they would have opposed. In 2008, they were also opposed and wanted to maintain the 2004 structure, which they had opposed. And truly, this year they are against the changes that are being made, they want to have the structure of 2008 – of which they were bone-hard opponents! For example, the Progress Party and the Right were: opponents of the establishment of the Armed Forces Operational Headquarters at Reitan in 2008. This year, it's not a word to hear about it – it's said because it was a right decision, and what was decided works right. And – I’ll almost call it a fun, but it’s not a bad idea – in 2008 the Progress Party set up a fourth manoeuvring battalion. Where is that proposal now? So much hasn't changed in four years, has it? It is not, of course, a proposal, simply because it was – and is – a bad proposal. So it's just to prepare: what will be decided today will be the policy of the Progress Party – not this year, but in the discussion of the next long-term plan. Prior to the treatment of the long-term plan by the government and the Stortinge, the head of defence did a thorough job through his professional military council. In particular, I want to commend him for putting forward what are the challenges of our time and the new threats of our time, such as: Cybertrussels. The Chief of Defense's advice, in addition to a number of other engagements, formed the basis for government and government party work on the long-term plan. However, it crystallized some issues where we did not follow the advice of the defense chief. We would not put down the 2nd battalion in the brigade, we would maintain one control and warning station in the Air Force and concluded that it would be in South Travel. We wanted to keep all of today's Orion surveillance aircraft, and we wanted to strengthen the area structure of the Home Guard and maintain the district losses in the Home Guard at Rygge, Terningmoen, Setnesmoen and Drevjamoen, as well as the HV school at Dombås – all of which were proposed to be closed. In addition, we would be clear that the defence will contribute to the co-location of their health services in the new centre to be established when the current Trom military hospital is laid down. When we discuss defence issues, there is often a lot about battalions, squadrons, vehicles, vessels, aircraft, bullets, and powder. It is well and good and also has to do, but none of this works without personnel. So let's not forget the people of such a complex organization as the Defense is. Without humans, none of the systems will work. The government has announced that it will present its own communication to the Government on personnel and competence issues. That's good, because this is a very important topic: what kind of competence does the defence need in the future, and how is this competence to be recruited? What kind of personnel changes will the organisational changes that we are adopting today entail? The protection obligation is one of the main defence foundations. If we take away the duty of protection, we get a different defence and a different society and not least a different relationship between people and society. In addition, we write in 2012, and then continue to discuss, investigate and assess whether we should have a female protection obligation or not, is not very forward-looking. In the notified communication of competence, we should now take the step fully and adopt a decision on the principle of female protection. We have protection obligations, and we have women. Then we should also have female protection obligations. The notification of competence must also review the current command system. When we visit the Departments of Defence, we get many feedback that we need to do something about today's command system. In particular, the feedback is clear when we get into the departmental command system. Here something has to be done. So on to that question in the long-term plan that will probably be given the greatest attention in today's debate — the question of fighter aircraft. It is very gratifying that Norway is fully committed to the acquisition of up to 52 new F-35 fighter aeroplanes. On 6 June 1975 the Stortinget decided by 100 to 19 votes to purchase 72 fighter aircraft of type F-16. It was a wise, correct and forward-looking decision. When F-16 arrived in 1980, it was a great technological and operational leap for the Defense. Norway is a long-distance country with large areas both at land and at sea and with similar large airspace. That's why an operational fighter weapon is extra important. New fighter aircraft are essential for the defence to be able to maintain our security and sovereignty satisfactorily in the future. Kampfly is a basic capacity in our defence structure. Fortunately, there is full agreement on that. The decision we are about to make today is as important and as historic as the decision on F-16 in 1975. From the decision to buy the F-16 was taken in 1975, and until the last plane is phased out, it is likely to take about 45 years. If F-35 could be used for as long as F-16, it's impossible to predict today, but in any case, the acquisition of combat aircraft is the greatest and most important single thing for the Defense in the foreseeable future. Then go to the base question. After thorough assessments of three current bases — Bodø, Ørland and Evenes — both as a base option and as two base alternatives, the government and government parties concluded that it would be right to locate future fighters to one base and that Ørland is the clear best location for such a base. This was in line with the defense chief's recommendations in his professional military council. I am pleased that the Right Party and the Christian People's Party are in agreement with the government parties in this, and that today – after all the sun brands – we can have a large majority for such a solution, where Ørland becomes the main base for fighter aircraft. The government and government parties also concluded that, with Ørland’s geographical location, a smaller operating base would be necessary in North Norway. After considering Banak, Bardufoss, Andøya, Bodø and Evenes' divorces, Evenes is clearly the best option, and therefore Evenes is proposed as a forward-looking base, because it is the best option – only for that reason. We don't make decisions about what's the best location of a forward base based on what's best for Bodø, or what's best for Evenes. We choose it from what's best for the Defense and best for Norway. Unfortunately, the right does not – the traditionally defence-friendly party. Old defense friends in the Right must be horrified at how their party has been lately. Unfortunately, the run-up to next year's election campaign has become more important than making good decisions for the Defense. For what is what is what the right do? For months and weeks we have been able to read that the Right would go for Bodø as a fighter aircraft base. But what do they do? Do they promote that? No, they believe that the main base should be established on Ørland, not in Bodø. But the much smaller forward base in North Norway, it will now have the Right in Bodø. So the right will put down today's fighter base in Bodø and replace it with a smaller operating base. Or are we so sure they mean it? Why have they not proposed it? No, they're going to have the government to figure out a forward base in Bodø. The government has assessed it and concluded that it cannot be recommended because the costs will be enormous. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e553	Every day, thousands of people do a very crucial job for our everyone's safety through their work in Defense. Defence must always be proportionate to the real security challenges we face. The starting point for defence should be our national security tasks, and SV believes that we should make good use of the opportunities for close cooperation here in the north. A commitment that focuses more on our neighbourhoods is in line with this thinking. The capacity to defend Norway and manage the Norwegian sea areas must be at the heart of the defence priorities. The government has received broad support for increased investment in the north, and it has long been an important part of SV’s defence and security policy. Climate change and changes in international power create new challenges and increase the strategic and economic importance of the northern regions. It calls for innovation in Norway’s international cooperation and security policy; it requires a strengthening of the links with countries with which we have strong interest. Nordic cooperation will, for example: provide significant gains both in terms of security policy, capacity and economics. It is essential for Norwegian interests that small countries are ensured by a well-functioning international conflict management law. Therefore, supporting the capacity and authority of the United Nations in conflict situations is an important part of Norwegian security policy. As a small country, Norway has an interest in supporting the ban on war by international law and maintaining restrictive rules on the use of force. Norway must therefore always require a clear and concrete mandate from the United Nations if we are to participate in international operations. Today it is government policy. It hasn't always been. Therefore, SV is particularly pleased that we are making it so clear today. Defence of our neighbourhoods as well as prioritising participation in UN-led operations are important ambitions in the new long-term plan. It is very gratifying that there is now a broad consensus on this approach to Norwegian defence policy. The most important task of defence is to form a war-preventing threshold that involves high risk and cost to the one who had to challenge our security and independence. Such a threshold implies that Norway has relevant means of power that can counteract pressure, aggression or attack. Defence will continue to facilitate the support of civil society in the event of accidents and natural disasters, serious crime and other crises. It is a priority we strengthen with this plan. The investment in fighter aircraft is Norway's most expensive mainland investment ever. SV believes that Norway needs fighter aircraft as part of the Norwegian defence system, and it is essential that a clear cost framework is set for the entire purchase and that the purchase is carried out in a way that ensures Storting’s control of the development of the F-35 programme and the price of the aircraft. It's not about any mistrust of the government, it's about having as much investment as this involves, we should have control all the way. That's why we're happy that the flight purchase is going to be dealt with annually in Stortinget, so that we elected people at any time have a hand on the steering wheel of the process. Nor do I feel that the government has any problem with it, as the government itself has proposed this approach to the flight purchase. The Swedish National Board of Directors has previously decided that when Norwegian fighter aircraft are considered out of date, it will be necessary to renew their capacity. Price was a very important factor in SV agreeing to negotiate with Lockheed Martin about the purchase of F-35. Calculations show that real costs do not differ from those presented to the Stortinget in 2009. It is essential for SV, so that fighter aircraft do not go at the expense of investment in welfare, health, care and school. Kampfly will be a central and important part of the Norwegian defence, and the costs of fighter aircraft must be seen in the context of the Defence's other budget. F-35 has a number of capacities that today's F-16 obviously don't have. There will be a new, high-tech platform for the entire Defense. In addition to being a next-generation fighter aircraft, the ability of the aircraft to obtain real-time and transmit intelligence and surveillance information will provide the Defense with new capabilities. This will provide a much better basis for both the pilots and the defence’s other network-integrated capabilities to make the right decisions in increasingly complex operations. Therefore, it is also natural that investment in fighter aircraft is part of the Defence's ordinary budget. The additional funding provided in the long-term plan is significantly lower than that outlined in the professional-military council. But we are sure that the defense can find good arrangements to cover the investment costs, even if we see that it will be demanding. The number of aeroplanes is important for the total cost and the long term plan provides for a reduction of the number of 42 aeroplanes plus the training aeroplanes. Many modern weapon systems can theoretically be equipped with nuclear weapons. It can fly, artillery, missiles, etc. I mention this because there has been a debate on the defence's long-term plan that F-35 could be equipped with nuclear weapons. But in order to deliver such weapons, the weapons must be integrated into the aircraft. This software will not be of interest to us, nor will it be available to Norwegian pilots. It should be completely unnecessary to point out that the nuclear weapon in Norwegian aircraft is an entirely, totally un topical issue, and there is complete agreement in the Storting. Norway has clear and clear targets for a nuclear-free world and is actively working towards this goal. The aeroplanes shall have a base, and the government has landed on Ørland as a choice of main fighter base. I believe that is a wise and correct choice, which was confirmed by the Committee's last visit to the base. I know Ørland well: there is little civil air traffic, there is little population density around the military base, and the possibilities for further development in a long-term perspective in the area are good. Besides, it's very nice there! Since Ørland is also the most reasonable option, it was right for the government to go along with this particular solution. Even so, it is the right place to locate a forward-looking operating base, based on both noise considerations, freedom of action, reach and price. It is worth noting that the Right does not join this whole solution, but the more interesting it is to follow the Progress Party in this case, which announces a rematch in the next period if the Stortinge's composition should change. I could think of feedback from the Right on how they will deal with this demand from the Progress Party, which creates great uncertainty. After all, we just got a very clear confirmation from the spokesperson, Jan Arild Ellingsen. For me and SV, it is important to promote a clear recognition of the veterans of Defence. The implementation of the Action Plan in service to Norway, presented last May, is well on track. For us, the protection of compensation for staff serving abroad is very important. The people who are willing to put themselves at risk for Norway should obviously have very good arrangements, and I am very pleased that the Ministry of Defence is following this well. All in all, the defence's long-term plan is a plan we're very pleased with. Norway needs a comprehensive and cross-sectoral defence and security policy, including an enhanced conflict prevention policy, to increase the robustness of key societal functions and to reduce the vulnerability of Norwegian society. The development of a comprehensive policy for increased security and security must be based on what needs to be defended and not on short-term threat assessments. Hopefully, that time is past when concepts such as the «UN-led world order» were debated in Norwegian politics, even though not very long ago. On the contrary, Norway is like a small country with large natural resources and an important location entirely dependent on a world society where the rules are followed and respected. With our geographical location and our large natural resources, we therefore believe that the following main tasks must form the basis of Norwegian defence and security policy: to prevent wars in our part of the world from actively contributing to building a fair international legal order to contribute to the disarmament and elimination of weapons of mass destruction to ensure democracy and the freedom of action of elected political authorities to secure fishing resources in the sea areas around Norway to ensure the production of our Norwegian pedestal oil and gas resources to ensure the economic and ecological stability of our neighbourhoods to ensure the Norwegian coast as safe transport years to ensure the infrastructure of society; I believe that the government's proposals for a plan, which we are discussing and adopting today, respect these priorities. Finally, I would like to thank the President-in-Office for his good management and for his good cooperation.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e666	In Norwegian security and defence policy, there is usually consensus on the basic framework. We in the Center Party are pleased that this consensus is being continued in this long-term plan as well. In the committee we agree on a lot and disagree on a part, but we have had good cooperation in the committee, and I would particularly like to thank the rapporteur for a good work. It's been a long and thorough job on this plan, and we've made great and important choices, which all the parties are behind: We will go to the purchase of new fighter aircraft. We are continuing the course for the Maritime Defense Department. We're going to continue to rust the Army to meet a new security picture. We have chosen to maintain a solid protection system throughout the country. For us in the Central Party, it is absolutely essential that we have a defence that matches resources and tasks. We believe that we have it today, and that we are going to have it further in the future with the long-term plan that we are now presenting. I, and more with me in this room, belong to the generation that was young during the cold war. The Iron Curtain was a concept, and that was the time we had an invasion defense, whose main function was to defend their own and allied territory against a clearly defined threat. The development of defence has gone enormously fast, and today's defenses are quite different. Development continues, and capacities that were useful yesterday may be obsolete or redundant tomorrow. This means that, as politicians, we must constantly focus on the capacities that we need the most and that best keep our defence structure up to date. The major steps taken in the defence’s long-term plan are primarily about changes in air defence. This is naturally related to the purchase of new fighter aircraft, which will be a huge economic boost for the nation. This has been the subject of a thorough debate in Stortinget in the past. The centre party is prepared to increase the defence framework in order to compensate for the costs incurred by the fighter flight purchase. However, I would like to stress that the defence itself, not least the Air Force, must be prepared to cover a large part of the costs within its budgets. Government Parties have chosen to take some structural measures that are difficult, but necessary, in order for the air defence to be able to recover a large part of the costs within their limits. The purchase of new fighter aircraft opens up in order to organize differently, not by increasing the reach, but because one has to do something with the base structure. After long and thorough consideration, and not without discussions in our group, the Center Party has entered into one main fighter base. We have then agreed that this base should be added to Ørland, which is considered to be the best option professionally. In order to secure Norwegian airspace, it is important that we get what is called a forward-looking operating base with QRA, Quick Reaction Alert, in the north, and this we want to add evens. Adoption of the phase-out of the combat aircraft base in Bodø after 60 years has not been a decision that we have taken lightly. Especially in relation to all the employees in Bodø, this is a heavy decision. In this context, it is important, first and foremost for individual reasons, but also in the interests of a strong need for competence for air defence, to take care of personnel at this difficult stage. This also applies to Rygge and Mågerø, which are other places where military activity is phased out. There is a great deal of uncertainty about such changes as we face, and it is important that the changes take place in as gentle a way as possible, and that a person takes the time to implement the changes. As the Defence is withdrawn from Bodø, it is important that the areas released can be used quickly for civil purposes and that that transition also takes place as smoothly as possible. It is also important for us to strengthen the environment in Reitan by moving there, with staff, the Inspector-General of the Air Force. It has also not been easy for us to go in for the closure of Rygge and Mågerø, but it has been a condition that the environments in Reitan and Sørreisa should be strengthened. Decisions on closures and relocations are always very difficult. The presence of defence around the country has not only been important for defence purposes, but in many places in the country it has also had a major regional policy significance. As a center party, I have a special commitment to community, and it is therefore not with an easy heart that we have had to accept that closures have been necessary. This has been a demanding choice, which has been and continues to be painful for communities. But we think they've been necessary to make the base structure even better adapted to the modern response defense. It is a little paradoxical that the whole committee is united behind the view that Norway needs a constantly up-to-date defence. But it doesn't just keep saying it – action must also be followed up. Here the government parties have dared to make demanding choices and have made some grips, as some places are, mildly, unpopular. It's not the opposition's willing to – while in many other contexts it's asking for action from the government. The grips that government parties are now taking will help the defense to deal with the huge acquisition of combat aircraft financially. The opposition parties go in to buy as many fighter aircraft as the government, but what is strange is that they do not take into account the sustained increase in operating costs that this purchase entails. That's why I don't get the opposition's calculation to go up, when they're not willing to take the structural grips that cut costs in the defense. Government parties want to give priority to defence rather than infrastructure maintenance. Now that we have made such decisions, we are left with a defence where consistency between means and tasks is good. As a representative of the Hedmark Armed Forces, I am naturally very concerned with the Army, and for the Central Party, the volume of the defense is important. We were therefore critical of the proposal to put down the 2nd Battalion and are pleased that this did not become a reality. The Chief of Defence's advice led to a sharp turn away from the defence-based defence. We were sceptical about that in the Center Party. We believe it is important to continue the duty of protection as base pillars in the defense, and are therefore satisfied that the government undertakes to continue the number of guardians approximately at the present level. We believe that this is important both for political and professional reasons. It also contributes to the consolidation of defence in the population. Our modern response defense is simply dependent on a solid protection system in order to function optimally. We are pleased that there is broad political support for maintaining the duty of protection as a pillar of defence, but we are concerned that the protection obligation system is also adapted to modern defence, and that the number of guardians admitted to initial service must be adapted to the needs of the defence. The land defence model set out in this long-term plan ensures both broad protection-based recruitment and gripability, while also ensuring the recruitment of HV. I believe that this is a solution that is well adapted to the modern Norwegian defence system. The centre party is particularly concerned with Homeland Protection, and for us, the most important priority has always been to secure a defense with a presence throughout the country, which has the means to train and practice. It was therefore very important for us to maintain the number of HV districts and a HV-force of 45000. We don't want to reduce either the number of soldiers or districts, as the head of defense suggested. We have that clearly, along with the other government parties, and we are very pleased with it. We are also pleased to note that there has not been any eagerness in the other political parties to cut drastically in the forces of Homeland Protection. Home security is today the only part of the defense that has a presence throughout the country, and it's also that part of the defense that most of the inhabitants get a relationship with during their lives. The significance of HV, both practical in crisis situations and symbolic in everyday life, must not be underestimated. What happened on July 22nd last year shows how important it is that we have an active HV that is present throughout the country. The number of enquiries we received from concerned people all over the country, but especially in the regions where HV districts were proposed, testify to a large and popular commitment around HV. Today, we are adopting a new long-term plan where very important choices have been made for the future of defence. It ensures protection-based defence with a presence throughout the country and makes very important choices for the future of air defence. The Government Parties hereby provide a responsible plan for the defence of the future, in which the balance between financial resources, structure and the tasks assigned to it is ensured.

We want a military professional and economic assessment of the different base options – something we don't think was done to a sufficient degree when Evenes was elected. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e879	The long-term defence plan links the past and the future. The generations before us have ensured that Norway has had a defence adapted to its time challenges. It has provided security for our country under changing security conditions. We're going to ensure a defense for our time and put the staf stick in the hand of the next generation. It requires responsibility, foresight and determination. The security policy image is broken. New powers are emerging, and while our own continent is hit by a deep and lasting economic and social crisis, there is a global shift of power, not least against Asia. In Norway’s neighbourhoods, global warming opens up to a gradual ice-free Arctic, with great opportunities for both cooperation and conflict of interest. The vulnerability of digital space is increasing, and cyberspace is increasingly appearing as a new independent dimension in our thinking about what defence is. With such complex challenges, a modern and adaptable defence is essential that can build a war-preventing threshold. Compared to most of our European partners, Norwegian defence is in a very privileged situation. We build up there other cutters down. But increased budgets do not exempt us from using Community funds in a good way. Norway is also in a special position in how we have been able to convert and streamline defence in a period of good budgets. Many of our European partners have pushed forward similar challenges and are now forced to cut short-term and very dramatic, driven by the financial crisis. I would like to thank Mr Syversen for how he contributed a real orientation on what the situation is, and the people you hear about in the NATO context. Norway, for its part, has taken similar decisions because they are wise and forward-looking, not because we were urgently forced to do so. The Defence Manager’s own annual report for 2011 provides a good illustration of how the actual level of activity of the Defence Agency is now increasing as the demanding but important transition to an intervention defence has been largely implemented. Over the past ten years, alternate governments have taken decisions that were controversial there and then, but have proved to be ahead of the future. The Armed Forces Operational Headquarters is located on Retan outside Bodø. We have assembled the Army with a centre of gravity in Inner Troms and one in Østerdalen, the Maritime Defense is gathered in Bergen, in Sortland and in Ramsund. There they are, and there they must be. It creates peace and predictability for defence workers and their families, and it allows for strong investment in these areas. It is very rare – as the representative Myrli reminded us – that people want to have back the facilities that were laid down, even though it was very controversial also in this room when it happened. Now it's the Air Force's turn. Air defence is facing an equally necessary process in order to prioritize its operational activities as the Maritime Defense and the Army did in the past – and has now completed. Let me remind you that during the treatment of the current long-term plan, i.e. St.prp. No 48 for 2007-2008, a unanimous Defence Committee stated that reform action was also necessary for the basic structure of the Air Force for the long-term and sound management of defence capabilities. I quote – from an overall committee in 2008: ‘The air defence continues to be influenced by an inheritance representing a cost-intensive diversification of activities. [[...]] If this is not changed, the costs of maintaining the bases will continue to draw resources at the expense of the operational capability of the defence. The Committee therefore shares the Government's view that in the future, air defence should continue the scope of its capabilities, but concentrated on fewer bases, on the basis that equal professional environments are co-located where it is rational.» These were wise words. Now we're there. We are the ones who are going to make these choices. In Norway, we have a long tradition of a broad political consensus on the main lines of defence policy. I am pleased to note, as the chairman of the Committee on Legal Affairs and Citizens' Rights did quite correctly, that there is also a consensus in this crossroads on the vast majority of points. The most important decision of this long-term plan concerns the acquisition of Norway’s new fighter aircraft of type F-35. This is an investment of no less than 60 billion. kr. The amount is almost identical to what we presented in 2008, and I am happy to note that. The unanimous nature of the Committee's position on this issue is very important for a small country. We don't buy fighter aircraft just for the sake of Air Force – we buy fighter aircraft for the entire Defense. Because without a credible ability to control our own airspace, investments in maritime and national forces give little meaning. In a highly-intensity conflict in and around Norway, our ability to control our own land and sea territories depends on our control in the air. In this area, it's simply not enough to be a good number two. The battle lights, however, do not operate in a vacuum. The government's proposal to establish an air operation centre on Retan outside Bodø has to be seen as a logical consequence of the fighter flight decision. The air operation centre — also known as the NAOC — will aim to plan and conduct complex air operations. Only in this way will we get the full effect of the aircraft acquisition. In the view of the government, this should replace one of the two control and warning stations we operate today. So this is in line with what the committee meant by the previous crossroads. The government's view is that tactical air control and alerts are collected on CRC South Travel, while CRC Mågerø is shut down and the NAOC is established on Retan at Bodø. It was also so that at the previous long-term plan, we temporarily placed the Air Force's top management in Rygge, until we knew what the fighter aircraft structure would be like. We know that now, and we believe that this should be added to Bodø and linked closely to the new Air Operation Centre and to the Defence Operational Headquarters. Then I am pleased that all parties, except the Advance Party, support the Government's proposal that there should be one main base for fighter aircraft in Norway – one, not two – and that it should be added to Ørland. It is very important for the Air Force and its employees that they receive this clarification. Ørland gives us a very good starting point for developing the fighting weapons of the future. I also note that there is agreement to establish a smaller, forward-looking operating base in North Norway, and that the disagreement is limited to where this is to be located. Of the five considered alternatives to forward base – Banak, Bardufoss, Andøya, Bodø and Evenes – the government has landed on Evenes. This solution is operationally good, while requiring the least initial investment, and is supported by the Defence Manager and the Air Force Inspector-General. To Borghild Tenden, who said she had not received an answer to the cost: it costs 56 million. SEK in investment and 76 million. kr to implement that solution. It says in one of the many answers we have had the pleasure of transmitting to Stortinget, in the extension that we also said the same thing in the long-term plan. We want to strengthen the army's response and flexibility. In Troms, the second Battalion of the Brigade shall be maintained and included as part of an Arctic effort force. The armored battalion shall be further developed as a heavy armoured battalion, in line with the Chief of Defense’s proposal, with a significantly higher proportion of personnel recruited in combination with any required personnel. Telemark battalion also gets an element of protectiveness. We thus maintain a fully-fledged brigade structure in Norway and at the same time ensure the continued relevance of the protection obligation. In a matter of its own today, the government is proposing to carry out the greatest material acquisition of the times to the Army. These are 146 new and upgraded armoured vehicles, supporting vehicles, tactical drones and related logistics. This is nothing less than the Army's F-35 project. It represents a strong uplift for our land power and costs $9.3 billion. kr. July 22nd and the extreme weather in the West reminded us that we can also experience serious crises in peacetime, which put society's overall preparedness to the test. Although the main task of defence is of a security policy nature, it is very important that all relevant resources of defence are available and trained for interaction with police and civil authorities. We are therefore further developing and strengthening Home Security with a local presence, which can make important contributions both in security crises and through assistance to civil society. I want to commend the head of defence for providing solid professional-military advice as a basis for the long-term plan. The government has followed many of the recommendations, but has also made some alternative choices. Where our conclusions have led to higher costs, the government has systematically taken this into account financially. The long-term plan foresees an increase of 634 million in the operational budget of the Defence Agency in Norway. kr per year, compared to the defence manager’s proposal, by reinvesting money that has been previously committed to the mission in Afghanistan and which was actually going back to the Ministry of Finance. This will now be a lasting strengthening of the budget. I notice that the opposition has not made clear proposals for alternative solutions, but wants more investigations. The government’s recommendations are based on very thorough assessments and extensive use of external quality assurance. We've delivered the long-term plan. We provide Stortinget with a comprehensive supporting material, and as mentioned, we have answered a total of 291 questions from Stortinget – partly about what was mentioned in the long-term plan. I think there is hardly one relevant question that has not been asked and answered. It's a time for everything. Several investigations do not lead us further. What is now required is the willingness to draw the necessary conclusions. Policy is essentially about making choices.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e1560	I am concerned with Nordic cooperation, and for many years I have been given the opportunity to join the Nordic Council. I am pleased that there is a good development also in the case of Nordic defence cooperation. Interestingly, this initiative was taken by the Norwegian and Swedish Defence Manager as early as 2007, and was subsequently followed up by the Finnish Defence Manager. This was both forward-looking and innovative. This was followed up by the so-called Stoltenberg report, which former government councillor Thorvald Stoltenberg drafted on behalf of the five Nordic defence ministers in 2009 – a good and important document. Since then, Nordic defence cooperation has become increasingly important, and interest is high. By cooperating, small countries can more easily afford to maintain a timely and modern defence. Practical cooperation on exercises and acquisitions has already been established. Defence cooperation in the Nordic region is not just of a practical and concrete nature. This has also been given a political side. Last year, the Nordic countries agreed on a Nordic declaration of solidarity in which they committed themselves to being united in crises. It is also very interesting to note that Thorvald Stoltenberg’s proposal for Nordic air surveillance over Iceland is now being discussed in Sweden and Finland. Norway and Denmark are already participating in air surveillance over Iceland under the auspices of NATO. Should there be an agreement on a Nordic air surveillance over Iceland, I think we can see the beginning of an even closer Nordic defence cooperation. Let me make it clear that such Nordic cooperation will not replace NATO cooperation for us. But Nordic cooperation can complement NATO cooperation, and through Nordic defence cooperation we can build bridges between Nordic NATO countries and Nordic countries outside NATO. In this way, we can lay the foundation for completely new defence structures. This I think will serve the whole Nordic region in a good way.

As a result of that event, it was decided that a 24 Dingo 2 vehicle should be quickly provided, nine of which had to be prepared as a route-clearing vehicle. The acquisition of Dingo 2 as a patrol vehicle has been completed and Dingo 2 is used as it is in Afghanistan. On the other hand, as far as I understand, it is not finally decided that the defence should use Dingo 2 as a route-clearing vehicle, even if there is a precarious need for such vehicles in Afghanistan, and that two years have passed since this tragic incident made the need for these route-clearing vehicles apparent. As far as I can see, today's route clearance is done by applying almost manually along the way with metal detectors and outbound personnel. Although they carry protective equipment, it is not possible for the equipment to respond to the adverse effects of a heavy road bomb. The miners who carry out this work are also exposed to hostile protection, both because they go outside, and because they are seen as so-called high-level targets. This form of route clearance is first of all very time consuming, and it also labors very hard both physically and mentally on the staff. Route-clearing vehicle is a special vehicle equipped to find or detonate mines over which they run. The vehicle is designed to withstand any explosion. If my version of this is correct, it seems incomprehensible that Norway is taking so long to acquire or develop suitable road clearance vehicles. This is therefore mentioned in the Ombudsman’s communication. I want to challenge the Council of State to comment on the status and progress plan for the acquisition and use of road clearing vehicles in Afghanistan.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e1931	The case concerns the proposal of representatives of two party representatives of the State Audit Board for a separate examination of the acquisition process and the basis for the election of Joint Striker, JSF, F-35. The rationale is that the Riksrevision does not have the practice of carrying out such ex-ante audits; the issue of the size of the plenary session may require the Riksrevision to initiate specific investigations. I expect that the Advance Party will justify its position. However, there is agreement in the Committee that there have been overruns in some defence acquisitions and defence projects, such as the former. The Gulf/LOS project, and not least in the acquisition of frigates, which had been carefully assessed. It is also a unanimous committee which emphasises the importance of, and assumes that the government has control over, the cost development of the acquisition of new fighter aircraft. It is also a unanimous committee which assumes that the Storting is kept informed of any changes to the fighter aircraft project, and also indicates that the government is up to it. In a separate remark from the Socialist Left Party, the Centre Party and the Left, reference is made to NOU 2007:15, A strengthened defence, the so-called Siri Bjerke Committee, which points out that there has been a recurring problem in the development of the Defence, namely the lack of consistency between economic assumptions and actual economic developments during a period of planning. Therefore, representatives from the SV, the Centre Party and the Left say that they share the underlying concerns of the proposers, namely the danger of such exceedances repeating themselves as Norway is going to make a military purchase that, in total, represents the largest real estate purchase ever. Government parties also say that one records that the government has taken the signals and concerns about exceedances seriously. The submitted long-term plan, which has now been examined, shows that the government will extend the acquisition of fighter aircraft over several years than previously planned and that the acquisition will be split into annual acquisitions to be approved individually by the Stortinge in the financial year in question, rather than making one large order. The government parties have also chosen to include Barth Eide's last assurances of costs and progress in the acquisition of the new fighter aircraft in Stortinget on 30 May. It is the last insurance that was provided before the setting was issued. Finally, I would like to say that all parties, except the Advance Party, will stress that the F-35 project has been selected as Norway's new fighter aircraft, and therefore do not want to contribute to delays and uncertainties regarding the fighter aircraft project. However, this majority underlines that the further process in the acquisition of fighter aircraft must be more transparent, ensuring a broad and cross-political anchoring of the largest and most important defence acquisition of the next decade.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e1954	This matter has taken its time in the committee, and it shows in many ways that there are some tensions in this complex that should not be rejected so easily. I am in a way a little disappointed that no other party supports asking the Riksaudition to go through this acquisition process around what is Norway's largest public procurement ever. The background is, as the rapporteur explained to the extent, that it is not right to impose this work on the Riksrevision because it does not have the practice of carrying out ex ante audits. I think it must be due to some sort of misunderstanding of what this proposal is really about. The acquisition process is actually complete – we even make decisions today. We have chosen the supplier at a much earlier date after a so-called tender procedure, and today's decision also confirms that. However, the question is whether competition until that decision was effective and whether or not the State has complied with the law to be followed in relation to such acquisitions. The proposal is not – and I would like to stress this – a rematch on flight selection, it is not an attempt to reduce the further process or a contribution to delay the further process. It is simply an honest and honest desire to ask the National Audit Office to examine whether everything went right during the procurement process until the choice of aeroplane type. It is, in the opinion of the Progress Party, a quite common and natural part of the activities of the National Audit Office. The Eurofighter consortium withdrew from the whole process because they considered that competition was not real. The Swedish authorities have been particularly critical in their considerations of the Norwegian procurement process. It's written a very critical book with a number of claims about what might look like manipulation. It really makes me ask the reason why Storting will not know any more. When the decision on the selection of the aeroplane type was taken, Stortinget got a proposition of less than 15 pages. That was the decision-making basis of the Storting. If one is to be careful with so little information prior to this type of acquisition, it must be a prerequisite that one can have confidence in the preceding process. The main question is not whether there will be overruns or not. The main question is whether the executive has conducted effective competition in line with the legislation between the suppliers concerned, or whether the design, basis and process have been such that it was actually predicted who would become Norway’s next supplier of fighter aircraft. If this is the last case, one should be honest about it from the start and also change the legislation so that one could choose the aeroplane type from political or strategic considerations and not carry out a tendering process. But I have no suspicion beyond the statements that have come from other teams that there may be irregularities in this procurement process. After all, it would be very nice for all parties, the whole Biggest and our democracy to find out whether that was the case or not – and that would rather not be the case. But it is alarming that an overwhelming majority of the Grand Duchy, consisting of all parties, apart from the Progress Party, will not even open up such an objective review of this great and very important process. This is not a unique proposal either. Studies have been carried out on the acquisition processes and acquisition of new fighter aircraft in other countries, including: in Denmark and Canada – and normally we are all very concerned about the proper use of taxpayers’ money. That's why we in the Progress Party think it's a little strange that we're not as concerned about the nation's largest public procurement over time. With regard to the formalities relating to the proposal, there is no doubt that the Storting should be asked by the National Audit Office to carry out such an investigation. However, when this does not happen – because I guess we do not get a majority in favour of this proposal all of a sudden today, even if we should have it – it does not mean that it is a hindrance that the Riksrevision on its own initiative believes that it may be important to look at the procurement process related to the nation's largest public procurement. Normally, the Riksaudition has engaged a lot in the state's procurement management, and there's no reason to dampen that commitment when we talk about such a large purchase as is the case here. With that, I take up the proposal of the Advance Party in the vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e1976	This is therefore a proposal from the Progress Party to examine the basis for the choice of new fighter aircraft and to ask the Riksrevision to conduct a specific examination of the acquisition process. There has been unanimity on this throughout almost the whole process, which started early in the last decade, i.e. around 2001–2002. It is, as it also appeared earlier today, the greatest single thing in the long-term plan that has been under discussion, and new fighter aircraft – we have heard that the reason for it before today – is one of the most central structural elements in the future defence. The adjusted JSF acquisition plan balances the consideration of ensuring continuous national combat aviation with the balancing of the budgetary burden in the acquisition years. There was no disagreement about this earlier today. It was important to ensure real competition. A good job was done to get it done. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e2070	In this case, I am delighted to present and present a unanimous opinion from the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs. The National Audit Office’s Annual Report sets out the National Audit Board’s 2011 framework conditions, management system and target achievement. The Committee has taken note of, and supports, the fact that the National Audit Office’s vision is to contribute effectively to the control of the Grand Duchy and to promote good governance. It is in good harmony with what I see as the role of the National Audit Office in Norwegian policy and administration. In a democratic society, control of administration is absolutely necessary. The National Audit Office is the largest external control body in the United Kingdom, and it is crucial and important for the Storting that there is a continuous review of the management's ability and willingness to follow up the decisions of the Storing, not least because, as elected representatives, we are to uphold the rights and demands of every citizen to know whether the resources are being used effectively and in line with political decisions. I am therefore pleased that a unanimous committee believes that the annual report shows that the National Audit Office is working hard and focused on this ambitious objective of effective control and better management. It is very worth noting, when the National Audit Office has been exposed in these days, I would say, for public criticism, that those who are actually the subject of the investigations, namely the administration, are satisfied with the work carried out by the National Audit Office. The management experience that they have a good dialogue with the National Audit Office, and in the user survey carried out each year by the National Audit Office, 75 pst. of the administration that they feel that the Riksrevision has sufficient competence in the implementation of the tasks. In addition, it is good to note that the criticism raised by the administration in the last year’s report that the Riksrevision does not always present its findings in a balanced and comprehensive manner, during this year’s user survey, is completely absent. For our part, we are of the opinion that all the 13 administrative audit reports submitted to the Control and Constitutional Committee in 2011 are both revised and relevant. It is also worth mentioning that the Riksrevision has now changed the structure of its Document 3 reports as a result of a peer review, which recommended that positive findings should also be more prominent and that the Riksrevision should be clearer in its recommendations and in its areas of improvement. The Committee notes that the Riksrevision has a unique position towards the administration. In the Annual Document 1, which is the Riksaudition report on annual audit and control, grant audit was a priority area for the audit of accounts in 2010. The Committee stated that the central government had challenges and that there were errors in grant management. The Committee considers that the Riksrevision has had and has a very central role in this area: to ensure that errors are detected and corrected, and to ask the Riksrevision to continue to pay attention to grant management, even though we note that the departments themselves have contributed to the practice of grant management in line with the wishes of the Stortingen and their own guidelines. It is essential that the Riksrevision should be included in the reforms, development processes and plans undertaken at all times in the Norwegian administration. The Annual Report provides us with a good overview of a broad range of activities that are important for the Storting to receive information about in order to fulfil its responsibilities as a constitutional body. The Committee believes that it is important that the Riksrevision addresses issues that go across the ministry. A cross-sectoral perspective is both sensible and necessary, not least because policy areas are interrelated and because they affect each other. Secondly, it is important that the eyes of the National Audit Office are focused on the major reforms and efforts undertaken in key areas such as health and care, municipal service production and social security and preparedness, to name a few. These are areas that concern us all and have a major impact on the individual's living conditions and the possibility of life development. Finally, the Committee welcomes the fact that the Riksrevision focuses its attention on these areas in the years to come. It is important that the objectives of the Storing are achieved — and that is a prerequisite for a good society.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e2091	The National Audit Office’s notification of the activity is an important summary of the Riksaudition’s work and plans and a kind of milestone in the relationship between accumulation and government.
