The evening post has referred to the report and led a number of people to comment negatively on the work of the National Audit Office and the National Audit Office. I think it is exciting that we experience that there are discussions about the Riksrevision's work and working methodology. I also do not have the habit of using reports from Civita as the basis for my own political speeches in this Chamber, but this report therefore deals with what is the foundation, really, for this debate, with a slightly different view – I would say. The Civita note concludes, inter alia: that the Riksaudition has professionally failed competence, that the Riksaudition criticises the administration with a failing mandate, that the criteria for the administrative audit are unclear, and that others should actually carry out administrative control, while the Riksaudition should concentrate only on what they consider to be its core task, namely accounting control. In addition, some consideration should be given to sending management reports to the professional committees, since the Riksrevision reports rarely deal with matters relevant to significant constitutional issues, as they state in the report. I'll start with the last one first. These are serious charges against the Riksrevision. But I also think it shows some sort of misunderstanding about the relationship between the Riksrevision and the Stortinget. A model with control responsibilities of the technical committees has been tried in Stortinget in the past, with the result that one soon found that it was more or less pointless, because the control work was a kind of left-handed work. This happens, of course, because the main focus of the technical committees will always be and should be to find solutions for the future. It is also made clear in the note that the main task of the Control and Constitutional Committee is to deal with constitutional issues. It's an important problem, but it's completely wrong. Most of our time goes to control and not to questions of constitutional importance – I would have almost said. We monitor both on the basis of the National Audit Office's reports and on the basis of self-initiated control tasks. In fact, we have – and I think we should be proud of that – one of the most modern control committees in the world, and it's an ongoing move from other countries to see what they can learn, and it's not that small! Having one committee specialising in control activities has clearly strengthened parliamentary control of administration, in line with the Constitutional Law's intention and provisions, and the change in the administrative and accountability structure of society. It is therefore, in my opinion, a fallacy if one believes that there is better control of the management by entrusting control responsibilities to the professional committees. So the report has a couple of good points as to the importance of being clear on what a basis is such as the Storting's assumptions in implementing management reports. This is also a topic that has arisen in many discussions between the Control and Constitutional Committee and the National Audit Office, and I have to say that I already perceive that there are improvements and changes in this respect. Then a claim was made that the professional competence of the National Audit Office is failing. It's serious! I have to say I'm not sure how thorough preliminary studies have been done before that claim was made by Civitas. In 2011, the Riksrevision carried out the investigation referred to by the rapporteur and the feedback from the establishments audited showed that at least 75 pst. held that the Riksrevision has sufficient competence to carry out its tasks. In addition, it is clear that Stortinget is satisfied with the technical content of the reports. It also made it clear to the President of the case that he is now speaking, and that is actually even more important than the fact that those who are subject to control are satisfied. The Civita note also claims that the Riksrevision carries out its work and comes up with its criticism on the basis of a failing mandate, i.e. that there is a misunderstanding between what the Riksrevision actually does and what this House thinks it should do. Of course, it would be a crucial issue in the setting if the Stortinget were of that opinion, but the Stortinget has not had that opinion in any of the settings on the Riksrevision that I have seen. And it's after all up to the Storting to give the Riksrevision signals if one believes one is moving within or outside borders as the Storting has determined. It has not been the case so far, with one possible exception. It is the administrative audit of the Bank of Norway. In addition, as far as I have seen, the Stortinget has not pointed out that the Riksrevision is moving in a direction that the Stortinget believes is wrong. With regard to the importance of the parliamentary control, which we have now talked about, and which is also the subject of the Civita report, I became somewhat discouraged when I read in the note that they called for an assessment to move Parliamentary administrative control completely out of the field of work of the National Audit Office and beyond to what they considered to be an independent body, such as an independent body. Directorate for Financial Management. Civita is therefore proposing to lift parliamentary administrative control from the agency of Storting to the government's directorate. I'm afraid there's a pretty strong indication that it's hard to take these recordings seriously. Of course, the basis for parliamentary control cannot be moved from a large-scale body to the government's directorate. Then there is no longer parliamentary control – to say the least. It is also unfurnished — at least — when Civita claims that the Riksaudition is not subject to control, because, in addition to the dialogue held by the Stortinget through the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee, with opinions and assessments related to the Riksaudition framework, the external audit of the Riksaudition is carried out by one of the largest audit firms in the country, in addition to carrying out peer reviews carried out at regular intervals. And this year's annual report presents a peer review and the results of a peer review in 2011. Some people probably think it's strange, but I thought it was good to use this Civita note as the basis for the report on the Riksrevision's report on the business, because it really affects the same situation, and because I think it is important that we tolerate criticism of the Supervisory Authority of the Stortingen, but the criticism should therefore be promoted on a factual and thorough basis. In fact, it is quite clear from my post that I believe the level of precision in the Civita report is somewhat lower than what we are used to from the Riksrevision. Therefore, it is easier to base the Riksrevision report and notification of the activity in 2011 on the allegations made by Civita. Basically, our national audit works, summarizingly, well, as the rapporteur described in a good way. As in all other areas, of course, it is potential for improvement, including: may be more efficient and may provide several management audit reports to the Storting based on the extent of the Riksaudition. It is important that the Riksrevision is even more up-to-date. It is this, fortunately, that the administration is essentially following the advice and recommendations of the Civil Ombudsman and rectifying matters in line with the recommendations of the Civil Ombudsman. However, in recent years it appears that the administration does not bow to the views of the Ombudsman. In his annual report, the Civil Ombudsman has carefully explained some of the specific issues where this has happened, so I will not go into it specifically, but instead say a little in general. Today's arrangement, where the Civil Ombudsman makes an opinion, and not a new decision, which is really common to other Boards of Appeal, has as a fundamental premise that the Civil Ombudsman's opinions are followed and respected by the administration. If the administration does not follow the Ombudsman’s opinions, it may be appropriate to weaken confidence both in the administration and in the entire civil ombudsman system. If the Civil Ombudsman’s opinion is not followed up, the Civil Ombudsman has some kind of last opportunity to recommend complaints to bring an action. Such a recommendation means that the defendant is covered by its own costs, but the scheme has the weakness of not being able to cover the costs incurred by the counterparty in the event of loss of the case, despite the fact that the Civil Ombudsman has recommended the action. It has the consequence that many people are hesitating to sue the State, because the economic risk of the process is still too great. The cases in which the Civil Ombudsman has recommended that the complainant go to trial are often matters of a principle nature. If the trend in the future is such that the administration chooses more to ignore the Civil Ombudsman's opinions, then I and the Advance Party take into account that the Civil Ombudsman is thorough and takes it into account in its future annual reports and, if necessary, makes suggestions on how to implement measures that can correct such developments.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e2200	There is cross-political agreement on the need for secret services. The secret services make a very important contribution to our national preparedness, and it is essential that the services have the necessary room for action to make their work effective. At the same time, sufficient democratic control of the activity of services must be ensured, and until that time, we have the EOS Committee, which is making a formidable effort to investigate whether the secret services operate within the legal framework given in this Chamber. Therefore, I would like to start by saying that the main picture is that our secret services do a very good job, and that there are rarely any irregular relationships that are discovered. The dialogue between services and the EOS Committee appears to be good, and the services take signals from the sample very seriously. They should keep doing that. The EOS Committee shall continuously monitor the secret services in order to ensure that the EOS services do not infringe the individual’s right to privacy and wrong the individual. The Committee is pleased that the EOS Committee has not encountered any particular problems in the exercise of its control activities in 2011 and that the services have consistently demonstrated a high understanding of the control of the sample. It's actually a brand year this year: the EOS Committee has 15th anniversary this year and is the youngest of the Biggest Boards. In the annual report, the sample points to some key developments since its inception in 1996, which may be worth noting. The first is the legal development that has taken place in recent decades. In particular, this applies to the incorporation of human rights, such as: Article 8 of the EMC, which shall ensure the right to privacy and family life. If public authorities are to intervene in this court, they must have a clear and precise legal basis. In line with this, the EOS Committee has been concerned that the intervention of the EOS services in the right to privacy should be clearly enshrined in the law and that the applicable procedures should be followed closely. It's an important and proper focus. However, the monitoring has shown that, from time to time, Norwegian citizens are still subject to intervention without the necessary legal basis. I'll come back to it a little bit later. The second area of development pointed to by the EOS Committee is technological development, which is much faster than legislative development, and that it can create a number of service problems. Technological developments also make it necessary for the EOS Committee to strengthen the technical competence of sample work. This is in agreement with the Committee. The third area of development is that the staffing of the Secretariat is generally strengthened, so that the EOS Committee can now go deeper into some issues and issues and, if necessary, set up specific projects. This is good. In 2011, the sample sent two specific messages to the Stortinget, on the monitoring of Norwegian citizens by the Surveillance Detection Unit Group, SDU, and on POT’s methodology in the Treholt case. The Committee would stress the importance of the fact that the EOS Committee will continue to make use of the possibility of sending specific messages to the Stortinge to ensure that such cases can be dealt with in a timely and political manner here. The Committee has noted that the EOS Committee has launched a separate project that looks into registrations related to a few selected environments on which PST is associated for prevention purposes, and the project will be presented in a separate communication to the Stortinget during 2012. I'll say a little bit concrete about the individual services, and start with the PST. The EOS Committee has not found any basis for criticising PST for the use of coercive agents such as communication control, space listening, secret search, etc., with the exception of one case. In October 2010, it appeared in the media that the PST had conducted hidden television surveillance of a cellar shed that was disposed of by a person suspected of forming a terrorist association with two other persons. The EOS Committee asked for a detailed explanation of this monitoring. Despite the fact that the EOS Committee had received ongoing information on the development of the case from the service, the sample was not made aware of the fact that the cellar booth was watching television. The service was of the opinion that surveillance was on a television surveillance of an object and that the surveillance was not so intrusive that it needed legal support. Following the assessment of the EOS Committee, television surveillance took place in a private area and was of such a nature as to require authorisation. The Committee agrees with the EOS Committee that the cellar shed is a private place and that the nature of the person monitoring was such that the authorisation was necessary. With regard to the lack of information on the methodology used by the EOS Committee, the Committee agrees with the EOS Committee that the nature of the hidden television surveillance should have been such as to provide information to the sample. The Committee assumes that, in the future, the PST will provide the panel with information on the use of all forms of intervention, including those services that may be designated as operational measures. The Committee is pleased that the EOS Committee has not found a basis for criticising the PST for the processing and delivery of data on Norwegian and foreign nationals to foreign cooperating services. Similarly, there is a review of complaints against PST, where none of the closed complaints provided a basis for criticism of the PST in 2011. So I'd like to say a few words about NSM, the National Safety Authority. The Committee is pleased that the inspection of NorCERT by the EOS Committee did not provide a basis for further follow-up by the sample. The inspection of NorCERT showed that the department is well aware of data protection issues. Furthermore, the Committee has noted that the process and evaluations in comparable clearance cases vary considerably between the various clearing authorities. The Committee agrees with the EOS Committee that, on the basis of a principle of non-discrimination, this could be unfortunate. The difference may be related to the existence of 45 clearing authorities, where the scope of the case varies from no clearing case to more than 20 000 per year. Nor do clearing authorities have access to an experience archive showing administrative practices in this area. The Committee has asked the Ministry to consider the need for any changes in this area. Under the current legislation, the requested person is not entitled to have access to the minutes of the safety interview before the decision on safety clearance is taken. The Committee agrees with the EOS Committee that such transparency should be exercised in the interests of objectivity and procedural information. As regards the control of FSA by the EOS Committee, the Committee is pleased that the EOS Committee has not found any reason to follow up the FSA clearance procedure. The FSA is the country’s largest clearing authority, with more than 20 000 clearing decisions of a total of 28 000 clearing decisions each year. The Committee has noted that three complaints have been lodged with the EOS Committee against the FSA, all of which have been closed without criticism from the committee. The Committee is also pleased that, in 2011, the EOS Committee did not reveal critical aspects related to the FSA’s exercise of military counter-intelligence. In the case of the E-service, the Committee is satisfied that the prohibition of the collection of information against Norwegian citizens residing in Norwegian territory has been complied with and no other critical factors have been found in the control of the technical gathering activity at the Defence Stations. The Committee has also noted that there was one complaint against the E-service of the EOS Committee, which also addresses the other services, and that the complaint was closed without criticism. The Committee is also pleased that the inspection of the critical infrastructure protection department by the EOS Committee did not provide a basis for further follow-up by the sample. Briefly summarised — and necessary after this far too long article — the annual report of the EOS Committee shows that the secret services relate to the framework that has been drawn up. It's positive, and something we're satisfied with. The Committee's position is unanimous in all areas and is presented for debate.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e2289	The case concerns a proposal from four Right-wing Representatives, in which a requested government proposes to introduce a system of cumulation by roll-call elections, at the 2013 Grand elections. The introduction of the new electoral law in Norway prior to the municipal elections in 2003 provided an opportunity for voters to have a greater influence on personal choice. When discussing the possible practice of this in relation to the choice of economies of scale, it is a question of establishing a well-functioning electoral system, which must take some cross-cutting considerations into account. The parties have maritimeed thorough nomination processes and that process ensures that the top candidates have the necessary prerequisites and experience to enter into their demanding positions as elected representatives. The individual batches have also been given a clear look at the extent to which they should ensure that this is balanced, so that social and geographical considerations are also taken into account. The background to the proposal is said to be a desire to extend access to influence who represents one at Stortinget, so that voters can enter and individually influence who is to represent one. It's at that point there are different assessments. While the representatives of the Right want to address this and implement it at the 2013 Grand elections, the government parties and the Advance Party are in favour of asking the government to investigate changes to the electoral system so that voters can influence the order of the candidates of the parties, taking into account the ability of the parties to ensure the influence of which candidates are elected. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e2330	A good and effective electoral system that ensures that voters have an impact on their preferences in terms of both policies and persons is important in a well-functioning democracy. It increases transparency and perhaps also participation in democracy. And increasing participation in elections has all parties agreed, at least in theory. In my opinion, a good electoral system strengthens a real and legitimate regime of peoples. The proposal for a representative to discuss the introduction of a system of cumulation in the event of major elections was launched on 24 March 2010. The proposal has therefore been in the Committee for an extremely long time. The representative Anundsen said in a previous post that when matters stay in the committee for a long time, it was an expression that they represented some kind of tension. It is not possible to see in this case! As regards access to cumulation in municipal elections, it was introduced well over a hundred years ago in 1896. In 2003, the electoral law, as is known, was amended to that in force today, i.e. a lot or a selector may enter a name twice or more times. The selectors can then cumulate, either by selecting a name or by rewriting their name. In addition, they may include a name, a so-called slangger, from the list of another lot. A key element in the introduction of the new electoral law in 2003 was that individual voters should be given greater opportunity to influence personal choice. I – and Right – now believe that the time has come for a new electoral reform. That is why we supported the proposal more than two years ago. It's time for the voters now to have the opportunity to cumulate even when they choose to do so. It had, in my view, been a positive development for democracy. This is already done in our neighbouring countries, where voters are given an opportunity to influence the ranking of candidates. In Sweden, the electoral rules of parliamentary elections are such that a party makes a priority list of candidates in a constituency. You can vote on one list and give a special voice to one of the candidates on the list. The votes of the persons do not affect the distribution of mandates between the parties. The election settlement distinguishes between candidates who have not received a number of votes of at least 5 pst. by the lot number of votes in the constituencies. This proportion is now known as 5 pst. in Sweden, both by parliamentary elections and by regional elections. The system has recently been changed in parliamentary elections by going from 8 to 5 pst. But it's not right, as the representative Anundsen said, that this was done in Sweden because it was found that keeping to 8 pst would not increase the influence. You wanted to increase your influence by going down to 5 pst. That was the reason in Sweden, and it is also from the parties that are now voting against doing the same in Norway. What I am saying, in other words, is that we have a system known from Sweden – which is practiced in previous parliamentary elections, and which will be practiced in future elections – that could be used. In Finland, voters must vote for one candidate. Parties taking part in the elections must place candidates in order of priority in the constituencies and those wishing to support the party must vote on one of its candidates. The number of votes of the party is the sum of the votes of its candidates and the number of votes of the parties is proportionally distributed between the parties on the basis of these votes. The 2001 Electoral Committee’s position – so we are back in Norway – concluded that the Norwegian electoral system does not have the flexibility that characterises most comparable countries, which, in the opinion of the Right, could be improved by a legislative change, which the majority now sends to the investigation and does not vote for. A well-functioning electoral reform, which has been discussed by several speakers, must take care of some cross-cutting considerations. This may, for example: relate to the ability of the parties to ensure their influence over which candidates are selected as central dependants and wives on behalf of the lot. It is desirable that the batches also have a significant influence on this process, not least to ensure that the top candidates have the necessary prerequisites to fill the work. The lot’s access to pre-cumulation or deletion of candidates, or the affixing of ‘slower’ from other lots, are elements of an electoral system involved in this balancing process. The parties’ need for predictability and security of their top candidates must be balanced against the interests of the voters of individual and political influence. I think that interest is there – the experience from Sweden shows that it is – and there are not so many differences between neighbouring peoples. As I said, a new electoral system would be a positive development for democracy and would contribute to a more well-functioning electoral system. In the opinion of the Right – and our view is supported by the Christian People's Party and the Left in the Committee – this scheme should have been introduced by the 2013 Grand Election. I see that at this point in time it has been in the past, but the matter has been in the committee for over two years. An investigation could have been initiated, or the proposal could have been adopted directly or with an amendment that would enable it to be implemented at the 2013 elections. But there has not been any political will to do so, and if I can say so, there is probably a certain distance between Prime Minister Stoltenberg's repeated speeches, well-worded and good, after July 22nd last year, which said that Norway is going to face the situation of'more democracy' and'more transparency' and a concreteisation of this. A change in the electoral system could open up more transparency, and on the right-hand side, we have been willing – if desired – to change our proposal to follow the new Swedish system, i.e. by 5 pst. That would give voters greater influence than the old Swedish system, which we have proposed. Nor has there been any willingness to do so during the treatment. So there has been real political resistance, and a unwillingness, to the proposal. The representative of Kolberg expressed himself well to be ‘considered’ of the proposal. I can only say that in that way that I am sorry that the scheme cannot be implemented in 2013, after the treatment it has been subjected to in the committee, and I hope that an investigation is not a new way of laying down the proposal. You might have your suspicions about that, but let's hope for the best. I bring up this proposal from the Right, Christian People's Party and the Left.

I think it's necessary to get even more focus on good agronomy in agricultural production. I therefore think it is very good that the agricultural settlement now commits to establishing a system of graduated payment of replacement subsidies in case of parental leave. It's a wanted scheme from many young users, and it's very good that it now seems to be going in order. A majority of the Committee, all except the Party of Advancement and the Right, emphasises the importance of making the best possible use of Norwegian resources for roughage-based livestock farming. It is an important factor in exploiting natural resources and preserving the cultural landscape. It is therefore important to increase grazing subsidies within a framework of 22 million. kr and that there is an increase in the regional premium for meat. Norwegian producers today do not meet market demand for beef. The majority of the Committee therefore calls on the government to implement a separate strategy to develop measures for the increased production of beef. TINE announces the need to increase milk production. It's a signal that I think it's important to discuss with the parties to the agreement. I think it's important that that signal is taken seriously. In April 2011, the Matkette Committee came with its investigation into the conditions of power in the food chain. The selection points to major challenges in the Norwegian food market, including: related to the organisation and lack of competition in the food value chain and a significant shift in power has taken place in favour of the chains in the retail trade. One of the suggestions from the sample is that a law governing the rules for negotiations, known as the law on good trade, should be established. I noticed that this has had a wide connection, among other things. from the LO, the NHO, the Consumer Council, the Farmers’ Layer and the Food Suppliers’ Association. I am pleased that the majority in the committee joins in this and ask the government to start legal work with a view to establishing a law on good trade practices and to setting up a legal committee to investigate this. The majority also assumes that there is an objective for the law to be dealt with during the current period of ageing. Another aspect which is not an element of the agricultural agreement, but which is very crucial for agriculture, is the protection of imports. It's not a simple problem. It is important to have a knowledge-based approach to these issues. Customs level and border protection must be balanced in such a way that there are no unintended consequences in terms of consumer prices, commodity prices for the food industry and the RAC industry. This must be under continuous surveillance. However, it is essential that we safeguard the space for action in border protection, that we maintain a sufficiently robust border protection system at all times. Under the WTO Agreement, the country has the opportunity to have an import protection to ensure national food production. It is a prerequisite for ensuring the disposal of Norwegian agricultural raw materials, obtaining fixed prices in the agricultural agreement and making use of the resources available to us for food production. The majority therefore assumes that the government ensures a high level of import protection. The Committee points out that, in order to achieve the objective of the agricultural notification to increase food production based on Norwegian resources, it is essential to ensure continued volume production, bulk production, special meat and milk as a basis for agriculture throughout the country, and as a basis for a continued competitive and innovative Norwegian food industry. This is extremely important to establish. Volume production is essential for industry, and it is the basis for developing a special product that can increase value creation even at primary level. In addition, the Local Food Programme is an important stimulus and player. Otherwise, I think it is nice that there has been an increase in the allocation of bioenergy, as well as an increase in the total allocation for forestry to 204 million. kr. When we dealt with the agricultural notification earlier in the year, we assumed that in order to achieve the high objectives of increased income, increased production and continued agriculture throughout the country, yes, we also assumed that the agricultural agreement would be one of the pillars of this. We're in a country that's marginal in food production. We want the industry to be involved and influence its future efforts. A break in new and no, well, but in a longer-term perspective it is very important that the industry itself participates in influencing the direction and dimensioning of the various elements of agricultural policy. It's very important that we realize that this is not a pay agreement. This is an industrial agreement in which independent farmers engaged in food production in a country where food production conditions are relatively marginal have the opportunity to influence their own framework conditions and product prices. Norwegian farmers are thus in a special position that has this opportunity, and I think that we should have a development that ensures continuity, good recruitment and innovation in agriculture.

Here we see the annual support in 2011 values since the so-called escalation decision in the mid-1970s, which also coincided with the start of the Norwegian oil adventure. After the sharp increase in aid at the end of the 1970s, culminating in a total aid of 45 billion. in 2011 in the early 1980s, the aid has decreased and subsequently stabilised between 20 billion. kr and 25 br. kr per year. However, during this period there have also been an average of 2,600 fewer farmers per year — a two-thirds reduction for the whole period 1976–2011. We had the butter crisis, it started in October and lasted until April. Six months were spent to get it posted. You go in and you look at the co-operatives that don't have a living base, and you remove the quota ceiling, and you're going to increase it by 3 pst. What happens then? Yes, one accepts raising the ceiling by 3 pst., if it is not a joint operation and has reached 750 000 litres, or has a roof of 400,000 litres. This is not the right way to do it, when you can also read in the nation that you lack 30 million liters of milk. It can be seen that during the butter crisis, the quota ceiling on milk was lifted – the one that was left until 1 April, when there was enough milk. So now we're in mid-June, it's been a little over a month, and now you're missing 30 million liters of milk. It shows that this is not a common mistake, it is a system error. Something needs to be done about it, there needs to be a change in this. We also have an agricultural bureaucracy that surpasses everything. Here we can do big cuts. We have major tasks from the university and doctoral thesis that claim there is a place between 5,000 and 10,000 agricultural bureaucrats, some say from 8 000 to 10,000 agricultural bureaucrats. This is supported by the Norwegian Institute for Agricultural Economic Research. Nor has it ever been demented by the government. Then I'd say the one who's tiring, consents. We also see annual support in 2011 values, since the so-called escalation decision in the mid-1970s, that expenditure on Norwegian agricultural policy – paid by Norwegian consumers and taxpayers – is very high. However, in a socio-economic assessment of agricultural policy, expenditure must be offset against socio-economic benefits. The relevant question, then, is whether agricultural aid has given a high return to society compared with other good purposes, such as: infrastructure investment, generally better business framework conditions or saving to future generations. In order to assess the welfare impact of agricultural aid, we can begin by comparing actual policies with the guidelines of economic welfare theory for industrial policy. Norwegian agricultural policy today is largely about production, regional distribution and income policy. It is to a small extent geared towards common goods, the environment and active competition. It must now be said that production promotion is part of modern, cost-effective agriculture. It is difficult to streamline production without compromising the nostalgic nature of the cultural landscape or producing food without affecting the external environment. Nevertheless, there is just support directed directly at the volume of production that is the support pillar of Norwegian agricultural policy. Food security is another type of common good that can legitimise public intervention. Food safety and security of supply are distinguished. The safety of food can best be ensured through the control of Norwegian-produced and imported food, today under the supervision of the Food Authority. Security of supply at national level is about the ability to provide food to the population in all circumstances. At all times, the need for calories and nutrients must be met by sources of national production, stockpiling, imports and food aid. Traditionally, in this context, you think of a barrier from the outside world, i.e. a drop in imports and food aid. Even against the latter scare scenario, policies will ensure the highest level of current production at normal times. Furthermore, the entry of fish and plant food may be increased at the expense of less energy-efficient food such as meat and milk. Nor does production during normal times have to be similar to the crisis menu. Not least, one can rely on stocks of energy-rich and storage-rich products such as cereals and sugar. When you talk about security of supply, it's striking to me that you don't mention that today we export 38 million meals of fish every day. The party wants a different alternative from the government. We wish, among other things: to lower the duty on co-feed at the same customs rates as in Denmark. That's going to save 1.8 billion. kr. We want better depreciation rates for machines and equipment, which we want to increase from 20 to 25 pst. We want to add an investment grant with conversion funds of 1.5 billion. We want to have a depreciation rate on buildings that increase from 6 to 8 pst. - a price of 150 million. kr. We want to remove taxes on the sale of farm use. We want tax-free fund provision and investment through an investment fund scheme based on a forestry model. We want dividends for other purposes to be taxed as capital by 28 pst. We want to lift the milk and meat production ceiling. We want to change and liberalize concession law. We want to deregulate and abolish housing, compulsory employment, bans on sharing and maximum price for concessional agricultural properties. We want to open up alternative forms of organisation for operations – joint operations, ANS, DA, AS etc. We also want to create a BSU project to get to this. We also want to stimulate the investment in bioenergy, among others. to follow up St.meld. No 39 for 2008-2009 on 30 p.m. bio-waste in agriculture shall be included in the production of bio-energy, which solves challenges, inter alia. connected to the spread area. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e3953	What we are about to deal with now is a proposition on the 2012/2013 reindeer management agreement with proposals to amend appropriations under the 2012 State budget. On 1 June 1993 the Grand Duchy of Luxembourg approved the Main Agreement for reindeer management, St.prp. No 66 for 1992–1993, and authorised the Ministry of Agriculture and Food to negotiate with the Norwegian Reinbrukssamers Landsförbund – NRL – on annual agreements for the reindeer industry. Negotiations on the reindeer management agreement 2012/2013 were initiated on 9 January 2012. The parties reached agreement on a new agreement at the final negotiating meeting on 23 February 2012. The agreement applies for the period from 1 July 2012 to 30. ==References== The framework for the reindeer management agreement 2012/2013 is 104.5 million. kr. This is an increase of 2.5 million. kr compared to the current settlement after the adopted budget for 2012. It is in the matter of this one's between the government parties and the Progress Party and the Right, with partial support from the Christian People's Party to the government parties and the opposition. That's how it works. I then consider myself to be done with the case rapporteur section and move on to the views of the Progress Party. The Progress Party is aware that a new reindeer grazing convention has been negotiated with Sweden but is not yet operational due to a lack of ratification. We consider it unfortunate that a new convention has not entered into force, and sees it as important that the government helps to get ahead of the Swedish authorities. In this context, it is noted that the lack of agreement prevents Norwegian reindeer grazing districts from using winter pastures in Sweden. We will point out that today there are major challenges with the reindeer industry in the border regions of Sweden. Municipalities spend considerable resources on conflicts with Swedish practitioners who build cabs on the Norwegian side and do not deal with Norwegian law on planning and building and Norwegian laws and regulations. When we look at uncertainties in the interpretation of reindeer management laws versus planning and building laws in relation to reindeer management, this is a problem that has recently escalated. Reference may also be made to the conflict in the Lenvik peninsula where hundreds of pure people from Sweden have been illegally resident. The area within which they have resided is an area not recognised as a reindeer grazing area, which could also have created problems for the municipality’s water supply for the approximately 10,000 inhabitants located in the area concerned. The problems are proliferating, the level of conflict is increasing and it does not appear to have a future solution that can bring peace to all parties. The consequences of the lack of new agreement between countries are propagated throughout the county by the reindeer being moved to areas not defined as grazing areas. It is today the municipalities as a third party that get the problems trapped. I would like to point out the importance of the government intensifying work on a new agreement, and as soon as possible agreement with the Swedish government is reached. We regret that the government parties cannot support our proposal to prioritize ratification of an agreement. The Progress Party also considers that there is reason to ask questions about the extent of the loss of puree caused by predatory game. The current compensation scheme is based to a small extent on evidence of losses. Nationally, compensation of 25 pst is granted. by what is sought, which has been killed by the peace of robbers. Of the number being replaced, it's just 6 pst. is documented. It was budgeted for 2010 to compensate for the loss of reindeer due to the peace of the predatory game of 55,728 million. kr, while the accounts show 66,082 million. kr. There is reason to ask questions about the internal control of the reindeer management, as the Riksrevision has done on several occasions. When we are in the Riksrevision, the Riksrevision has also today produced a report that is directly crushing against the Minister of Agriculture. I would like to quote from the conclusion of the Riksrevision report: ‘The objective of ecologically sustainable reindeer management has not been achieved. Large parts of the Finnmark range are excessive because the reindeer is not adapted to the grazing basis. The last grazing survey in 2009 shows that more than 50 per cent of the low-covered area is low-bidden and about 40 per cent are reduced. For 90 per cent of the low surface, the low thickness is below the limit of when it is most productive. Pasturage resources are most impaired on spring and autumn pastures and on early winter pastures. The average slaughter weights and meat yield for the period 2006-2010 do not comply with the limit values for an organic and sustainable clean figure. These conditions are severe in all reindeer pastures in eastern and western Finland, with the exception of Polmak/Varanger. The purity figure has increased by about 40 per cent from 2002 to 2010, and the established purity figure from 2011 implies that the number of pure must be reduced by about 20 per cent.» The government was going down with respect to the number of reindeer. It has not been able to trade and not be able to do its job. Contrary to going down the reindeer, you get an increase of 40 pst. This is incredibly serious for the ecological basis in Finnmark. I take up the proposals of the Advance Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e3987	I am pleased that there was an agreement on the reindeer management agreement for 2012–2013. The frame's at 104.5 million. kr and there is an increase of 2,5 million. kr. We in the government parties are committed to ensuring a reindeer drift based on economic, ecological and cultural sustainability. We are very concerned that all reindeer operations should be well anchored in these posts. It's not like this today, completely. But I would like to stress that in the reindeer industry there are a lot of competent professionals in the entire area of the country that has reindeer operations. For Christmas last year, the Reinhriftstyrelsen had completed its work of approving the rules of use and the upper reindeer in all the pages and areas. The authorities now have a better basis for a detailed overview of the situation. I want to pay tribute to the Rein driftsstyrelsen for well done work. Some now have the opportunity to increase their numbers if they find it appropriate, while it is clear that many have to reduce grazing pressure in their areas, and it is important to make progress in this. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e4354	In connection with Report. St. 22 for 2011–2012, Growth Tools, we are dealing with government policies for the development of Innovation Norway and SIVA. Both institutions are important tools for facilitating good nutrition from the public sector. In the Right we have also met through our meetings with the business community on many sunny stories about the business meeting with these institutions. This room will also say that both Innovation Norway and SIVA have made a significant contribution to the development of innovative solutions and industrial development in rural areas. We in the Right have some critical comments on this matter. First of all, we believe that some measures need to be taken to improve the instruments, so that they will give Norway increased competitiveness in the future as well. Preparing for the future means, in the opinion of the Right, that we are facilitating the competitiveness of Norwegian business to meet the challenges of the global arena. With the high level of Norwegian costs as a platform, our business needs to be among the best technological and among the most efficient and innovative to compete. This is a competition we don't have to play qualifying rounds to get into. We're in an endless tournament game. Norway, with its business and modern public sector, is a technological superpower. We are also – measured against the vast majority – able to produce both rationally and efficiently. So it's only when we get to our innovative power that we lose terrain measured against other nations we should compare with. We have to take that seriously. This government and Innovation Norway uses the following definition of innovation: ‘a new product, service, new production process, application or form of organisation that has been launched on the market or put into production to generate economic values.’;» In the Right's view, the definition is good. It's descriptive, points forward and gives a good picture of why we need to focus on innovation when we talk about industrial policy. Therefore, we in the Right are surprised to note that it is not possible to include the Research Council and the research framework conditions and funding in the notification. Research and development is, in the Right's view, a very central and natural theme in an innovation debate. That's why we're wondering that the government can't see this in the context. When Innovation Norway was established in 2004, a number of cultures were merged to create a powerful tool for food and district development. Policies that facilitate innovation and policies that facilitate rural development can, but do not necessarily, be two sides of the same coin. In the right we believe that the message lacks a clearer distinction between these two. The geographical demarcation of innovation policy can, in the Right’s view, limit innovation, which is the opposite of what the Right believes is the objective of such a policy. At the same time, we believe that limiting rural development due to the lack of innovative target formulations cannot be a desired policy either. We've been able to describe both of these issues in several places in the last few years. On the right, it would stress that the objective of Innovation Norway is to contribute to knowledge-based growth and value creation. For this reason, consideration should be given to opportunities for projects that do not contribute to innovation to be added to another management. The challenges of innovation in Norway today, with a low level of innovation, are often important tasks that contribute to both conversion and development in regions and municipalities. In the right, we are well aware that there is a high level of competence in Norwegian municipalities in this type of project, for example under the BU Facility, and that it should be considered to decentralise such tasks – which will also help innovation in Norway to focus its activities on fewer and more innovative tasks. In the Right we constantly have to talk about taxes, and taxes are an important tool, also in such a debate. Using tax incentives to strengthen the innovative power of businesses is, in the Right's view, much more the right way to go. Development models based on the needs of enterprises and seeking innovation have better prospects for success than for enterprises to use resources to adapt to public programmes and processes. Therefore, the right supports the positive wording on the tax regimes in this Communication, but believes that these need to be strengthened and improved, as we have proposed in our draft budgets in recent years. The notification is unclear as to the incubation and early-stage capital instruments and how the State can strengthen its role in supporting and commercialising early-stage companies. The right considers that the nationwide start-up grants are a scheme that takes this stage and that has had an excessive reduction in amounts in recent years. In the notes to the right, the NCE programmes and the Arena programme, which are successful cluster programmes, are financed by Innovation Norway, while the Research Council finances the TTO programmes and the SIVA incubator programme. All these actors have instruments and grant schemes designed for early-stage projects. In the right, we believe that there is a need for better coordination of the instruments, so that they can be more focused on the most innovative growth enterprises. The right will therefore stress what the government itself writes in the communication, that there is a strong need for further development of the instrument portfolio and assumes that it will involve evaluations of the effectiveness of what is deployed by resources in the different types of instrument. Then to the seed fund scheme. The right is positive that new funds are being announced. Six funds are promised, and we record the capitalisation of two funds in the last revised budget. The right would point out that it is absolutely necessary that the new seed scheme should also be able to attract private capital. With the high risk to investors present in new innovative and internationally oriented enterprises, it is essential that the seed scheme be designed to attract private capital and competences. We are aware that the requirement is private capital, but the mechanism of the funds should also be such as to attract that capital. In the right, we are unsure whether the scheme for the proposed cereal funds – as reflected in the revised national budget 2012 with a reduction in the risk relief from today’s 25 pst. to 15 pst. – will trigger the necessary private capital, and considers that the government in its further work on the organisation of new seed funds must pay particular attention to the establishment of sufficient incentive mechanisms to trigger private capital. The right also points out that the funds should be set up in such a way as to enable the seed funds to take risks and invest in enterprises with an assumed undissolved potential. The right would also stress that regardless of the allocation of the management of the funds, the funds shall be nationwide in their investment activities. As I said earlier, it is regrettable that the research section has not been evaluated in such an important report. We also record that the government in the notification calls for a review of Innovation Norway’s instruments, anchoring a new target structure and the company’s portfolio of instruments. The right-hand share of the Ministry's view that this has not been addressed in this communication, but believes that it should have been. In the light of the strong criticism that came from the National Audit Office on the achievement of the objectives and use of resources in the company, we are also considered when we read that Innovation Norway itself should be given the task of proposing concrete simplifications of its own portfolio. This sounds a little bit clumsy. Summaries are that the Right also believes that we need more knowledge, and we are therefore looking forward to getting on the table the results of the notified evaluations. The lack of integrity of the knowledge base means that the Right does not go for the Left's proposal, No. 2 but here we would like to announce support for the Left's proposal, No. 3, on an enhanced incubation strategy, placed under SIVA. Finally, the Right will reiterate that the principle that companies themselves create and control their innovation, in many cases, provides a robust model. Therefore, the right considers that the tax FUNN scheme should be strengthened and that direct tax deductions should be granted for research and development investments. These are tax systems that are proven to work well and on which we need to invest. As said above, companies themselves are the best ones to define their needs for innovation and new ways of working in order to remain competitive. This is the Right-wing Reason for the proposal to lift the limit on hours and hours of self-produced research and development related to the TaxFUNN.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e4501	The purpose of industrial policy is to maximise total value creation in the Norwegian economy and work for all. The Norwegian economy is going well. We have good growth in the Norwegian economy – and significantly higher growth in 2012 than in the first quarter of 2011. The growth is shown in both the production of goods and services. The number of persons employed also increased by around 20 000 persons from the fourth quarter of 2011 to the first quarter of 2012. A number of economic indicators point the right way for Norway. At the same time, there is a trend towards a third of business. The part of the business that supplies goods and services to the oil and gas sector is doing well, while industries that depend on demand for other export products are divided between those that are doing well and those that are struggling. In recent years, the international economy has been unstable and the prospects for the future are uncertain. The crisis in the euro area has increased, with increasing government debt and unemployment. In some emerging economies, growth is still high and contributes, among other things. to maintain a high oil price. Norway is not unaffected by developments in the international economy. An OECD report presented in May highlights the potential for a worsening of the euro area crisis as the main threat to the Norwegian economy. Although the development of Norwegian business is mainly good today, and although today’s unemployment is low, we must constantly work for the business life of the future and for the value creation of the future. The government facilitates a business that is sustainable, innovative, knowledge-based and adaptable. Key industrial policy efforts go through the design of good general framework conditions. This includes, inter alia: a responsible economic policy, well-developed infrastructure, a good education system and good welfare arrangements. However, good framework conditions alone are not sufficient to trigger maximum value creation. That is why we need to complement the general framework conditions with business-oriented instruments, including: through Innovation Norway and SIVA. Innovation Norway and SIVA manage high values and take decisions of great importance for entrepreneurs, businesses and business environments. There are strong requirements for the management and operation of the companies and there are great demands on the achievement of the objectives of the companies. In addition to the annual follow-up and reporting to the Stortinget, the government has carried out extensive company evaluations in recent years. This was notified in St.meld No. 7 for 2008-2009, An innovative and sustainable Norway. The evaluations, the consultation rounds and the recording conference organised in the context of the work on this communication show to a large extent that Innovation Norway and SIVA manage their tasks well and that the results are good. Nevertheless, there is always room for improvement and for further development of companies. In this Communication to the Government, the Government draws up the lines for the further development of Innovation Norway and SIVA. The Communication presents new objectives for Innovation Norway and for SIVA. Good and clear objectives are necessary to ensure good governance and easy reporting. The new objectives clarify the achievement of the companies and lay the basis for a more overall governance of the contracting entities. The new objective of SIVA underlines that the company should be the country’s instrument for the organisation of ownership and development of enterprises and the business and knowledge environments throughout the country. It is also stressed that SIVA has a special responsibility for regional growth. The main objective of the SIVA is to trigger profitable business development in enterprises and regional business and knowledge environments. These objectives shall be achieved by the SIVA through its property activities and its innovation activities. The SIVA objectives underline, inter alia: that the company has a role to play in major industrial projects. The Government is committed to the future contribution of the SIVA to the development of several major property projects of local, regional and national importance. For the purpose of Innovation Norway, it is emphasised that the company should be the State and county authorities’ instrument for realising value-creating business development throughout the country and that the main objective is to trigger business and socio-economical economical economical development throughout the country. It will be achieved by working for more good entrepreneurs, more high-growth enterprises and more innovative business environments. The notification draws up the guidelines on how the State can exercise its ownership of the companies. An overview is also provided of how the owners of Innovation Norway will jointly follow up on how the company works in strategically important areas. In addition to following up the simplification work, the owners will follow in particular how the company meets the customers, how the company works in the field of internationalisation and how the company plays the role of adviser to owners and contracting entities. The Communication contains a number of measures that will strengthen the efforts of Innovation Norway and SIVA for Norwegian business. Innovation Norway’s instrument portfolio should be simplified. The company currently has many schemes, and it may be difficult for companies to find out about relevant support options. The meeting of the companies with Innovation Norway and SIVA shall be simple and provide rapid clarification. At the same time as the company's portfolio is simplified, the ministerial management signals must also be coordinated and a common target and performance management system for all contracting entities must be established. This will make it easier for the company to report, and the contracting entities and the Storting will be better informed about the effects and the achievement of the objectives. The notification contains several early-stage measures for access to capital. Among other things, the notification announces the establishment of up to six new seed funds. The revised budget proposes a framework for the first two Funds. It is intended that each fund should have a total capital of approximately 500 million. kr. The Communication outlines a more flexible mandate for the Investinor. The investor currently has a total investment capital of SEK 3.7 billion. kr. In addition, it is announced that the ownership of Investinor is transferred from Innovation Norway to the Ministry of Industry. The Communication announces the development of the offer of early stage capital and the development of the offer of pilot and demonstration projects. I will also give Innovation Norway a mandate to support the establishment and development of business angels' networks. This group of investors will be able to benefit from networks linked to seed funds or incubators. Many Norwegian companies compete in international markets, and internationalisation is therefore a central part of innovation in Norway’s activities both at district offices in Norway and at foreign offices. In the Communication, it is clear that the government will strengthen the company’s work on internationalisation. The presence in Asia and Africa should be strengthened and cooperation between the actors in the instrument should be improved. A separate Strategic Council for Innovation Norway’s international presence shall also be established. And there will be a new function – Invest in Norway – that will handle in a professional and active way enquiries from foreign companies that are considering their establishment in Norway. The government is working for a fully-fledged and competent instrument that has relevant and effective offers to the Norwegian business community. This Communication reviews the objectives, working methods, cooperation and instruments used by companies to assist Norwegian businesses in meeting the challenges we face in the coming years.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e4759	The Progress Party has proposed the creation of an ICT-hazard Commission – in line with the model of the Aviation Commission – to identify the cause of State ICT-hazards and ICT-related data protection breaches, and to add an existing body to its administration. In the light of the proposal, the proposers point out that the Norwegian authorities collect a large amount of information on the individual population. The proposers point out that data protection is not respected and that the authorities are leaking information to unauthorised persons. In the proposal for a representative, the proposers are in favour of an ICT-hazard Commission modelled precisely from the Aviation Commission in order to limit the adverse effects of the opportune treatment of sensitive personal data by the public and to identify the cause of state-owned ICT-hazards. The Committee has pointed out in its examination that the proposal concerns a number of worrying issues and considers it important that central government should give priority to privacy considerations. The proposal describes a number of problems with public ICT solutions and data protection challenges. There may be relationships with the tax authorities, the transport sector, the police, the emergency network, the health sector and Nav — examples from these areas underpin this type of issue. The Committee has pointed out in its consideration that the proposers believe that a Commission for ICT Hazaris and ICT-related data protection offences could prevent such problems from arising in the future. The majority of the Committee points out that the proposers do not mention the existing body to which they wish to add this Commission, but notes that this can be done within existing management. The establishment of a fixed breakdown commission, which is intended to cover such a large and defective area, is not necessarily the right medicine for the prescribed diagnosis and symptoms. It may be most appropriate to involve private actors in carrying out quality reviews and assessments. Here we can only refer to the Ministry of Industry's commitment to the Norwegian Veritas for the assessment of Altinn. At other times it may be best to use existing governmental bodies, where the Ministry of Consumers and Administration has used, among other things. National Safety Authority for the assessment of the safety in the Government Quartet. Flexibility is necessary. Improvements and good, up-to-date control and follow-up procedures are always needed. The majority of the Committee, consisting of the Workers' Party, the Right, the Centre Party, the SV and the Christian People's Party, points out that the use of ICT solutions by the State covers a very wide range of areas and that it could be difficult for a single body to deal with all situations of injury and privacy. The government announced a big message about privacy next winter. Several of the issues raised in the proposal will be discussed here. In addition, in spring 2012, the government will issue revised national guidelines on information security, and we should probably be there by now. The majority propose that the proposal be annexed to the Protocol. So to the views of the Right, where we stress that new technologies provide opportunities for also necessary innovation in several sectors. In the area of care for the elderly, technological solutions can also provide greater security and help more elderly people to live longer at home. Advanced safety alarms, so-called smart homes and new opportunities for online healthcare can help improve the quality of care for the elderly. The public must play a role as a demanding and competent demand for services using new technologies. Public shall and may stimulate innovation and thus improve resource use, inter alia. the care of the elderly by requiring service providers, whether public themselves or private service providers, to apply new technological solutions. It is important that the public take seriously its service function towards its citizens. In addition, it has a public responsibility for data protection. There is varying quality of digital provision in different parts of the public sector. It is important that departments and agencies also take this seriously, and that service is part of a culture and a system and not dependent on individuals. In addition, it is important that robust IT solutions are prioritised and that central government has secure computer systems. Public data on own activities and administration should be as accessible as possible to citizens, as this will make it easier for organisations and businesses to develop services. Today and in the future, it will be a matter of course for the consumer that information is available, about available children's playgrounds, health homes, etc.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e4782	The proposers address an important issue in this Document 8. However, when the majority of the Committee, the Red-Green parties and the Right, have chosen not to support this proposal to set up their own ICT-Hazari Commission, it is related to the fact that we believe that it will not be a good enough measure. The use of ICT solutions by the country covers a very wide range of areas, and it will therefore be very difficult for a single body to deal with all adverse and privacy situations. Problems that will arise are different and often require very rapid action. There will then be a question of a disaster commission that will cover such an extensive area could have sufficient efficiency to deal with such a situation, as the rapporteur was also concerned. She also pointed to the example of the Ministry of Economic Affairs that engaged the Norwegian Veritas for the assessment of the ALTIN – this for the fastest possible assessment of what had caused the serious security breaches. I would also like to mention that we already have more supervision today that is responsible for some of the issues raised by the proposers. The data supervision is both supervisory and representative and has an important function in ensuring that personal data are kept safe. Serious breaches of the personal data law security provisions shall be notified to the supervisory authority and supervision may impose the correction of any errors and deficiencies. I therefore support the Council of State in – as it emerges from her letter to the Committee – that there is already a system to protect privacy when failure of the ICT systems leads to accidental disclosure of data of value for protection. The Post and Telecommunications Authority also has a role as a disaster commission for events in the ecosystem. Providers shall report supervisory outcomes and events. The Postal and Telecommunications Authority then assesses what has happened and needs any action, and reports on it to the Ministry of Transport. Technological development challenges us on privacy. Sensitive information about us is handled and stored by a number of agencies, both public and private. Violations of the security provisions that result in unauthorised access to personal data can have significant consequences for individuals and are unacceptable. I am therefore very pleased that the government is working on a communication to the Stortinget on privacy. The notification will affect both the applicable laws and developments in the field of privacy. The Communication will also address the challenge — and potential — of developing new information technologies. This will give the Storting a good opportunity to have a broad review of the privacy challenge we are facing.

NOK, and now they're over 750 million. kr, i.e. a tripling of the costs of introducing an ICT system into the State Road Service. ICT problems in the courts have led to the rewriting of judges who have disappeared from the computer system because of the loss of protocols. A well-functioning emergency network is the lacmus test of whether a modern society is able to secure its citizens when accidents and injuries occur. Our point is not to hang people out, but to get the Norwegian public sector to start learning from all the mistakes that have been made in ICT projects. The main reason why ICT projects in the public sector often fail is that ordering skills in the public sector can be too poor. The party believes that it is high time that the Norwegian authorities began to learn from all the errors that have been made in public ICT projects. ICT systems are becoming increasingly complex, and more and more knowledge is required, which the public sector may seem to be lacking. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e4833	The representative, Mr. Bård Hoksrud, has taken up the proposal he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e4836	It is positive that the Storting is committed to getting more out of ICT investment in general government and, at the same time, to strong privacy. The representative proposal presents a number of examples of challenges facing tax authorities, the transport sector, the police, the emergency network, the health sector and Nav. I believe that the proposal deals with two main issues. One concerns the planning and implementation of ICT projects in the public sector at the agreed time, quality and cost. The second concerns the strengthening of privacy. In April, the government presented the digitisation programme Online with its citizens. It contains common objectives and strategies for digitising public administration in the years to come. The management shall provide comprehensive and user-friendly digital services. The services shall be based on a secure infrastructure which shall also take into account privacy considerations. In order to develop the digital services of the future, we need, inter alia, to: continue to develop and use common components such as: Altinn, electronic ID, Folkeregisteret, Matrikkelen and the Device Register. Good security and privacy are important in this work. In addition, laws and regulations need to be adapted for digital communications, as demonstrated by the Government’s digitisation programme. The government will shortly present new national guidelines on information security. ICT security will also be addressed in particular in the forthcoming Annual Report of the Ministry of Justice on Social Security. Quality assurance of public ICT investment is done in several ways. The Ministry of Finance's quality assurance scheme for large investments is an important contribution. My ministry is now sending annual circulars with overall demands for ICT investment in the state and projects promoted are reviewed separately by the Ministry of Renewal, Administration and Church Affairs. Divi has a project guide.no for both large and smaller ICT projects. Some ICT investment has greater uncertainty and risks than other projects. That's why we're doing an extra job improving these things. At the same time, a single technical challenge cannot stop the development and deployment of new technologies. Increased use of technology can pose challenges for citizens' privacy. One option that we want to exploit is the technology itself. This applies, inter alia, to: solutions for electronic access to personal data. The point is, as the Committee is in its position, the use of privacy-promoting technology and embedded privacy. Privacy shall be an element of development from the start. There is one of the core elements of the concept of ‘integrated privacy’. I agree with the Committee that the management must plan for privacy when ordering and developing new ICT systems. In the announced privacy deposit notice, the government will among other things. discuss the use of privacy-promoting technologies. Although the objective is good, in my view, a permanent ICT-hazard Commission will not ensure the necessary efficiency and quality in a work that requires different approaches to different issues and issues. In some cases, it is most appropriate to involve private actors in carrying out quality reviews and assessments. Several have referred to the Ministry of Economic Affairs' use of Norske Veritas to assess Altin, and the Ministry of Renewal, Administrative and Church Affairs has used specialised consulting services to assist us in the quality assurance of the Ministry's ICT services. At other times it may be best to use existing governmental bodies. And as the representative Schou mentioned, the Ministry of Renewal, Administration and Church used the National Security Authority to assess the security of the ICT system of the government quarter because they had the best available competence. Such flexibility is necessary. At the same time, it is important that we be able to act quickly, and there should not be any uncertainty about liability. I note that the majority of the Committee points to the fact that the use of ICT solutions by the State covers a very wide range of areas and that it would be difficult for a single body to take care of all the damage and privacy situations. That is also my position.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e4864	The Progress Party wants an efficient public sector that improves from year to year. Improving and reorganising the public sector requires that you are able to learn from your mistakes so that you do not make the same mistakes over and over again. This is why, among other things: The State Disaster Commission for Transport, which has contributed for 20 years to improving safety and reducing accidents in the transport sector. I have no doubt that the State Emergency Commission on Transport will succeed in its work. Every year, the state spends enormous sums on ICT projects, but a large proportion of the projects over the last 20 years have fallen apart. Then we have to ask ourselves how we can avoid ICT-hazards in the future, and how to learn from the mistakes that have been made. The Progress Party has therefore promoted a proposal for a representative on its own disaster commission for ICT, which has also been addressed by ICT-Norway. Creating such a disaster commission could in the long run save taxpayers for billions of dollars in public misinvestment in the years to come, even if a bet could avoid any misinvestment for the long term. The main reason why public ICT projects often go wrong is that public procurement skills are too poor. Government changes specifications and requirements during the process. Too much attention has been paid to new developments rather than to the choice of tried and tested solutions. In addition, a distinction should be made between what is important and what is less important in contracts. It's e.g. a little bit special with a situation where a consulting company can resell the Altin, which, after all, costs about half a billion dollars to develop. I am very disappointed with the way in which the Minister for Renewal rejects the whole proposal in his reply to the committee, which is attached to the vote. Governments say, among other things: that the establishment of a permanent accident commission will not cover such a large and defective area as the use of ICT in central government. What does not come to light is the solution that the Council of State itself has for the public sector to be able to learn from ICT-hazards. So it's for the government to shoot down a good proposal from the Progress Party without coming up with good alternative solutions. You can ask yourself who is most familiar with ICT – the ICT-Norway trade union, which has called for an ICT-Hazari Commission, or the Red-Green Government. Finally, let me pay tribute to the Christian People's Party, who has chosen to support our proposal. It is important that the civil parties find good common feasible solutions for improving the efficiency of the public sector, so that taxpayers are given the best possible services for their tax money. Here we are at the very heart of civil policy.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-14.ana.ud1886e4879	The representative Haugland says that the government and I have not shown any solutions to the issues raised in the proposal. Then I would like to remind you that what is the best solution to avoid getting ICT-hazards is exactly what I have explained in my answer – that we ensure quality of ICT projects in a way that avoids problems in retrospect. It has to be the best way, and that's the way we can learn how to avoid having major problems with our ICT proposals in the future. We need to build on some common standards and some common principles to link projects and ICT systems. The fact that we don't have systems that relate to each other is one of the things that challenge us most at the moment.
